- **D191 - Dress rooms with an isolated, self-checking builder; D119 applies to every dressing mesh (November 3).** The Decks and Tallow were large grids of one floor plate and Sorrel wide flat plains. `RoomDressing.Apply` rebuilds only a `Room dressing` root per scene (stand-ins are switched off, never deleted), rebakes that zone's navigation, and refuses to save a layout that blocks any replay-route corridor, door, speaker, spawn or encounter arena, removes a prompt approach that existed before dressing, or lets any dressing mesh rise into the fixed camera's sightline to reachable floor (`SightlineAudit`, exact meshes, the Hushwell sweep's chest point and near stop). With a 40° camera this caps collidable pieces on open floor at about 1.4 m; taller pieces stand against the full-height back wall, inside closed rooms, against an excavation face or in front of other furniture. Sorrel's slope and verge scatter is generated and pruned by the same rules instead of failing. The four Decks cabins were open through side gaps behind closed hatches; generated low walls close them (a MAP_EYE_TEST construction fix, with its own negative-control test). Ground variation on Sorrel is terrain vertex colour plus strata limited to steep faces through a new opt-in shader mask, so no vertex, collision or navigation changes. Floors get walkway runners on real circulation and rugs in homes, office, galley and refuge. Rerun the builder after any rebuild of these scenes. Final in-player MAP_EYE_TEST remains open. [Evidence](validation/C8-room-dressing.md), [art ledger](art/C8-room-dressing.json).

- **D192 - Walkway centres get a flat detail layer; lamps light their pools; Sorrel's basins get plants, tracks and ground (November 3).** Pass 1 left the open middles of every hull (the circulation) as bare repeated plate, 1.3 m stick lamps that read as specks, and Sorrel's basin floors plain. Circulation cannot take furniture, so `RoomDressing` adds a detail layer that lies on the floor and therefore cannot block a route, door or sightline: amber guide studs along every runner, stencilled pictograms (dock, office, cabins, market, galley, repair) inside each room's threshold, hazard paint at dock doors, hull separation collars and work/cargo bays, and inset grates, hatches, drains and tread plates on the plate grid. Details are alpha-clipped generated decals batched per kind and 8 m cell, so each batch takes its own nearby lamps under Forward's per-object light limit; they avoid rugs, runners, walls and furniture so nothing z-fights or hides underneath. Stick lamps become waist-high bollards with glowing tops and a real warm point light on the stations; Sorrel's bollards stay unlit, because its terrain is one renderer and extra lights would fight the per-object limit across the basin. Wall-side pipe runs, junction kiosks and cable drums go only where the camera sees them and no replay corridor runs (Tallow gets no kiosk: every open spot near its lobby is a route). Sorrel gets soft, alpha-blended ground patches whose kind follows the terrain colour's clay/dust zones and the ore bodies, tyre tracks on Chaikin-smoothed haul lanes, low boulder groups (clearance and sightline pruned), survey beacons by the ore, and alien plant clusters under 1 m in the band within 30 m of the roads. Plants have no collider, so they ignore replay corridors but stay off roads, out of encounter arenas (D131's combat-lane rule), and clear of prompts and spawns. New Sorrel pieces and decals stand on the terrain only: the first build draped tyre tracks over rocks at 2.5 m and set a plant on a roof, both caught by `SightlineAudit`; `RoomDressingDetailTests` now requires every detail vertex to lie on the real ground, with a floating-decal negative control. Organic scatter is decimated to ~1,000 triangles. Route protection is per segment: a segment that only runs into an interactable (the ore node or console a route walks up to) is an approach and is skipped alone; a segment through a wall marks the whole route obsolete. Dropping whole routes had left live chain routes unprotected. Final in-player MAP_EYE_TEST passed for the Decks interior, Tallow and Sorrel by agent review. [Evidence](validation/C8-dressing-pass2.md), [art ledger](art/C8-dressing2.json).

- **D193 - Hushwell and the Gullet get dressed by the same self-checking builder; the cave is lit in chunks (November 3).** Hushwell had one dim uniform floor on all three levels. The cause was structural: the cave was a single renderer, so Forward's six-lights-per-object limit let only six of its ~20 lamps, vents, crystals and organ glows reach the floor at all (D189's "practicals author the look" never happened). `RoomDressing` now copies the cave into 16 m lit chunks (materials per submesh kept) and switches the whole-cave renderer off; its collider, navigation and every gameplay object are untouched, and an EditMode test proves the chunks draw exactly the cave's triangles. With the lamps working, Hushwell's fill rises slightly (ambient and exposure) instead of staying tuned dark. The floor carries what each level is: on the miners' level, rails from the breach to both ore galleries with the two loose carts now coupled on the line and a buffer stop at the scaffold, warm work lamps along the haul tunnels (the galleries are lit by their crystals), the miners' camp at the breach (power cell, cable drum, bench, core rack) and their cache in the east pocket; in the pressure gallery, a pale scald crust exactly as wide as each vent's 3.2 m plume, so the dry ledges read at a glance; on the far walls, crystal spars with their own cyan light, lighting the galleries and the descent; in the nursery, seven cradles of three kinds (full, hatched, single egg) and different sizes clustered asymmetrically off the replay paths and clear of the three pools, instead of five identical cradles in a ring. Floor vertex colour follows level and use (dusty warm above, cooler basalt in the pressure gallery, damp blue-violet below, darker at the walls, paler on the worn route). The Gullet is a flight zone: everything on its bed is collider-free and stays below y=-0.6 against the ships' plane at y=1 (`FlightAudit`, plus an EditMode test with a negative control). Its bed now shows the anatomy its brief describes: vessels running with the passage, digestive pools and pores in the feeding chamber, scar tissue where the throat folds rub, silt settled in front of the eddy's wreck, egg clusters thickening toward the nursery gate, and the coil scoured in arcs by the serpent's body on its flat bottom and fold crest. Builder fixes found on the way: Hushwell's seals carve its navigation when the data is added, so prompts behind them (nursery, lift, cache) were never protected and the sightline sweep skipped the nursery; the builder now opens the seals for the whole run (as Hushwell's own bake does) and restores them before saving (prompts 1 -> 4 protected, sweep 11,044 -> 17,211 standing points). The reachability probe's search radius (`at.y+1`) went negative below y=0; it is now a fixed 2 m. A refused patch no longer reserves floor. In the cave, patches end at the foot of a wall instead of being refused, and rails and patches test the real rotated colliders, because the grooved walls' axis-aligned boxes reach metres onto the floor. Final in-player MAP_EYE_TEST for both maps remains open until captured traversal is reviewed. [Evidence](validation/C8-dressing-pass3.md), [art ledger](art/C8-dressing3.json).

- **D194 - Skills are worth spending charge on; ordinary enemies grow more dangerous through the chapter (November 4).** A single-target burst skill (Arc Cleave, Ember Dash, Lance) cost three landed hits of charge plus a four-second cooldown and dealt 1.7-1.8 times one hit, so using it barely beat attacking; the `SkillDef.damage` fields were never read. Bursts now deal `CombatActor.SkillBurst` = 2.6 times the actor's output on the ground and in flight; fan, pulse, net and the overdrive buffs are unchanged. Ordinary enemies dealt the same damage in Hushwell and the Gullet as in Sorrel while the party grew stronger, so a new `ZoneDef.enemyThreat` scales non-boss outgoing damage per zone at spawn: the Gullet 1.2, Sorrel and Hushwell 1. Hushwell was first set to 1.25, but the chain showed its Scrapmite bursts already take the agent party to about a quarter of its integrity, and at 1.25 Taren fell in the pressure gallery (`Builds/quality/C7/nov04/s08`, kept), so it returned to 1; in the Gullet the party never dropped below about 65%. Bosses keep their own phase scaling and health: in the last chain they already took the agent's party to 20-30% integrity, and longer fights would outrun the replay budgets. These are agent-measured numbers, not accepted balance; human play decides the final tuning. `EnemyThreatTests` failed on the committed code first.

- **D195 - The first town's morning plays on a genuine New Game (November 4).** STORY_CAMPAIGN's opening (Taren repairing a market walkway, a fruit seller's stall over his safety line, Sela arriving with a receipt for a ship that already left, the station shifting a few centimetres) existed only as an uninstalled October draft. It is now built from that draft, streamlined: only the Title's New Game sets `opening.marketRequested`, so Continue, every save made before it and every controlled fixture are untouched, and New Game still begins in Halo flight. Docking at the office reveals the office-to-market bridge closed by two amber gates with a service cover open in its deck (`MarketOpeningStaging`, isolated) and Mira's mobile cart, a generated `DressMarketStall` kept under the 1.4 m sightline rule, across the yellow line. Sela comes across from the market as Taren reaches the job (`ScriptedWalk`: authored blocking, not navigation; it stops for pause, blocking UI and anyone in its way, and is checked against the real scene when staged), the repair conversation (`SelaWalkwayRepair`) plays with a camera tremor and hull creak on the line about the bridge moving (`<<stationShift>>`), the cover closes and the gates fold back, Sela walks on to wait for Orrin and Mira returns to her sales bay. Orrin stays unavailable until then, so a premature talk cannot mark him met and skip the conversation that brings Sela aboard; `Npc` and `DiscoveryPoint` now treat a disabled prompt as unavailable even to a direct request. A walking civilian with a measured stride profile plays its walk at that profile's speed. The chapter-01 route gains the scene by insertion, every earlier step unchanged. The stale `docs/writing` dialogue copies are synced to the shipped Resources text, which a world rebuild would otherwise have reverted. Not built: hands-on repair animation, a physical receipt, Mira's measured walk.

- **D196 - Exteriors get dressed; the cave floor ramps softly; the dormant Cantor coils; Tallow's line reels (November 4).** The Halo's three hull roofs were bare repeated plating and the Tallow refuge's deck a stretched, soft hull texture under the flight camera. `RoomDressing` gains both: roof equipment on the Halo (each hull's trade painted large on its roof, solar arrays, tanks, pipe runs, hatches, hazard frames around the plant housings, a comms mast, corner lamps), collider-free and checked by `ExteriorAudit` to stand on a roof above the flight plane with the same roof continuing 2 m past every footprint; the refuge's deck takes flat crisp details only, under the Gullet's flight-ceiling rule, using temporary survey colliders on the collision-free hull. Hushwell's floor material uses six toon ramp steps instead of three: with three, floor in the walls' shadow quantised to no key light at all (the lower ramp read black) and every lamp or crystal pool was a hard two-ring disc; the rock keeps three. Seam crystals now light the ramp's shaded east side. Gullet strip details fade out over their last metres (vertex alpha) instead of stopping square, and grooves worn by the current lead the eye to the exit aperture. The dormant Cantor rests in a loose S with a hooked tail, joints 2.2 m apart as in motion, breathing until its head moves, inside every existing preview constraint; the moorings stay (moving them means regenerating the coil's sockets). Tallow's docking line is a generated braided rope tiled along the line instead of a flat ribbon, and once the party has seen its free end (`tallow.lineObserved`) the reel winds it in along its own path until the fitting rests beside the reel, where the Keeper's return conversation describes it; a later save restores it wound.

- **D197 - Civil movement jogs, sprints keep their pace, and the first morning answers (November 4, Nathan's report).** Nathan played the new opening with his Logitech Precision and reported three things: walking and running looked like wading through a swamp, a sprint that changed direction dropped to walking pace although the button was still held, and no NPC would talk to him. His log shows the pad's interact button working (it docked and launched him twice). The NPCs were the opening's own gating: Orrin, Mira and Sela were switched off until the walkway repair was finished, and the repair was only offered once Sela had finished crossing the market, so a player at the barrier stood in front of silent people. They now answer with short holding lines (`Npc.holdNode`: no "met" flag, no quest, so Orrin's briefing that brings Sela aboard still happens), and the repair is offered as soon as Sela sets off; she finishes her approach while its first lines play (`ScriptedWalk.walkThroughDialogue`). The swamp look was a speed/clip mismatch: the owned walk is a 1.1 m/s stroll and the owned jog only reads as running near its own ~6.4 m/s pace, but civil travel used a 2.6 m/s "walk" (cadence pinned at 1.7×, step stretched 1.39×, hips lowered up to 23 cm) and a 5.4 m/s sprint (the jog played at 0.85×, slow motion). Civil movement now jogs at full tilt (4.5 m/s: the jog at a slightly quicker cadence with a shorter step, never slowed below its own cadence), walks under half tilt (1.5 m/s) and sprints at 6.6 m/s (the jog at its own stride); the gait threshold moves from 3.1 to 2.5 m/s, a walking knee keeps a 2 cm bend reserve (the stride-1 landing locked straight and snapped at ~1230 deg/s), and free running leans into pace and acceleration and banks into turns at the lower spine, after the legs are solved. Sprint: civil sprinting no longer spends Thrust at all, a spent combat sprint waits for 30 Thrust instead of flickering on and off every few frames (which averaged near walking pace while held), and a turn at speed now swings the velocity's heading instead of blending through a straight line (a 90-degree turn kept 71% of its speed; a near-reversal still brakes). Flight boost is unchanged.

- **D198 - Boss fights mark their ground, grow by phase and open up when they should (November 4).** Nathan asked for boss fights in the spirit of Ys, Elden Ring, Zelda and top-down shooters. The three chapter-1 bosses each had one or two specials telegraphed by a ring of sixteen particle puffs. Every boss special now lays an exact marking on the ground (`Telegraph`: circle, ring, cone or lane, filling from its origin as the tell runs, crisp at the edge, conforming to the ground; flat under the ships in flight) and strikes only heroes inside that marking; a dodge still evades. Phases grow the set. **Burrower** (three phases at 60/30%): a marked charge that sticks fast when it rams rock (exposed for 2.6/2.2/1.8 s: bait and punish), spoil lobbed into marked circles, a tail sweep for anyone behind it; then a dive that hunts the active hero from below and erupts (exposed afterwards), double charges, and in the last phase seismic rings to outrun or dodge through. **Bellows Below**: its inhale now draws the party in before the exhale ring, its organ blast marks its half of the chamber, its ribs slam out in a marked star; pressure bubbles drift after the active hero from phase 2; the phase-3 rib crossing is unchanged. **Cantor**: the choir rhythm and every collar-link effect stay as built; once its integrity falls it weaves rotating spiral song (gaps to fly through) and, last, a coil lash along a marked lane. These follow integrity only, so cutting the collar never unlocks them early. Crossing a phase threshold mid-fight plays a shift: the boss braces (1.5 s untouchable), shoves the party back with a marked ring and brings in what the next phase adds (the Burrower calls two Scrapmites, which leave quietly when the fight ends). `Telegraphing`/`TelegraphRemaining` now describe the next strike that threatens the active hero, which is what the companion and the replay driver already read. A boss bar (name, epithet, integrity with a lagging chip, phase notches, break gauge, "opening"/"changing"/"below ground") and a title card replace the lock-on line for the boss. Not built: new boss animation (the moves are staged with the existing bodies), boss voice.

- **D199 - Chapter 1 gets side stories that leave something behind (November 4).** The chapter had one gather quest. Three side stories now run through the people already there, each advancing only on its own conversations (`ObjectiveKind.Flag` steps set by `<<setFlag>>`, never "talked to X"), never taking a first meeting (`SideStories` routing requires the speaker met), and paying out once: **An overdue parcel** (Neve held one of Sorrel's missed deliveries; the Survivor's reply comes back; seeds Neve's return-address thread), **Quiet ridges** (after the Burrower, the Survivor asks the party to silence four survey beacons still calling machinery toward the outpost; they blink until silenced and stay dark on every later visit; frame plating from the outpost stores) and **A berth for two** (Oda's Nacre berth token sits on Orrin's desk; line nine hundred and four of one harbour's single door seeds the Nacre disaster). Quest rewards now go where their kind lives (parts to gear, gels and cells to the item bag); before, everything landed in materials. Each quest step has player-facing text in the quest log, and a task card under the objective panel announces new, updated and completed tasks once the conversation that caused them closes. The chapter now ends on a beat: once Tallow knows it is moving, Ilo of the Compact survey reads Director Venn's words on the beacon band (food, power and a route that waits; show me), which keeps Meret herself for Act II and needs no new portrait.

- **D200 - The script speaks like its people, and screens never show an id (November 5).** A review of every line found the chapter's older scenes written without contractions ("I am coming", "Let us see the stock") beside newer, natural ones, a few cryptic exposition lines, an all-neutral Tallow scene, and two key people still called "Survivor" and "Keeper". Every chapter 1 node was rewritten in its speaker's voice (cast notes in `GAME_DESIGN.md` §3) with the same nodes, commands, flags, options and line counts, so the replay routes and story tests still hold. Yarn speaker ids stay stable keys; the dialogue panel, barks, NPC labels and prompts show `CharacterDef.displayName` through `DisplayNames` (the foreman is **Dace**, Tallow's keeper **Keeper Abri**). Items, materials, zones and targets are named readably everywhere (`DisplayNames.Item/Zone/Target`), quest titles are title case with log descriptions, the pause menu and title use device glyphs instead of literal "B"/"Y", one term per thing ("survey key", "quest"), and a locked warp's prompt returns to its destination once the route opens.
- **D201 - Skills are learned with Sync level, equipped four at a time and mastered by use (November 5).** All eight skills shared identical numbers and were all available at the start, with nothing to learn while levelling. Each hero now learns four more at Sync 3, 5, 8 and 12, each with a distinct job taken from games that do it well: Taren's **Anchor Slam** (a leaping, invulnerable gap-closer that breaks guards), **Tether Hook** (a hookshot that reels small enemies in and staggers them, or pulls Taren to big ones), **Forge Spiral** (a travelling five-hit spin for swarms) and **Faultline** (a ground rupture that reaches 10 m and stops at walls); Sela's **Survey Drone** (a turret that keeps firing for nine seconds while she moves), **Waypoint Blink** (an invulnerable blink that leaves a charge behind), **Gravity Fold** (a well that drags movable enemies together, ticks and implodes) and **Starfall** (a marked circle that beams fall into after a warning). Every one has a flight variant. Four are equipped (RB + face / number keys) from a new **Skills** pause page; a skill already in a slot trades places. Mastery rises with each release, Ys style (levels at 6, 18, 36 and 60 uses: +10% power and −2 Charge each). The companion now spends Charge on in-range equipped skills every six to nine seconds. The Sync cap rises from 10 to 40 (chapter 1 ends near 7). The original eight keep their exact behaviour; `SkillDef.kind` replaces the slot-and-character switch. Icons are a second generated sheet styled from the first. Not built: an Ys-style EXTRA super move.
- **D202 - Chapter 2, The Lantern Road (November 5).** Built from the atlas's Draft 3 order (Lanternwick follows Tallow) as the answer to Venn's third condition, "a route that waits". Abri remembers that Lanternwick's lanterns once guided moving stations; the party warps through **the Wick Reach** (space combat: Muster raider skiffs, the Choir swarm they stirred, and two dead relays to relight before an amber light curtain opens the dock), reaches **Lanternwick** (a canyon town of carved terraces, lantern towers, an arch market, an archive and an orchard; Keeper Aumi distrusts visitors who come back for the glass), goes down **Lantern Gorge** (open ground: light-shy Gloomgrazers that relit relays slow, a Muster scavenger camp, three relays that open the buried door) into **the Sunken Relay** (a dungeon carved from the canyon rock, a checkpoint less than a minute from its boss) and duels **Hessa Korl**, a Muster scavenger captain: the first person the party fights rather than a beast, with a combo whose finisher is held a beat, a grapple chain that leaves her open if dodged, a sidearm, a sweep against crowding, crew calls at each phase and a lantern flash to outrun. She yields and is arrested, not killed. Aumi gives the relay chart and sends Pell to keep a lantern on Tallow; Abri closes the chapter by pointing at food and Xylos. Three side quests (Pell's lamp, Ishen's record plates from Oriel, rations for Brekk, an Unsworn deserter) foreshadow the Muster, the Bridles and Oriel. Six new people have generated 16-expression portraits; the town's bodies are reskins of existing rigged civilians, the Muster's of the Sentinel Husk, the raiders' of the patrol cutter, all with original UVs. Twelve generated kit pieces (lantern tower, arch, canyon house, column, great lantern, relay mirror, lamp post, orchard tree, lantern shard, Muster crate, lean-to, cutter rig) and two generated canyon textures. The terrain is a heightfield whose cliffs are their own mesh with strata mapped along each wall. Lanternwick plays Old Village. Not built yet: chapter 2 replay routes (left to the QA pass), ordinary town residents, new boss animation.

- **D203 - The Gullet runs one way until the Cantor is free (November 6).** Nothing brought a party that had gone through the Gullet back to chapter 1's open quests. A warp from Tallow Approach to the Halo now opens on `bossdown.Cantor`, and the outer warp says plainly that the Gullet runs one way, with a free repair tender beside it. The QA audit found the stranding; the gate keeps the first passage a commitment.

- **D204 - A loss costs a walk from the door, and a save is never spent by accident (November 6).** Every boss door is a checkpoint: starting the fight moves the respawn there and saves. "Return to last autosave" brings the party back whole, and an old autosave that holds a dead party loads standing. With a save on disk the title focuses Continue, and New Game asks first with Back selected. Loading an old slot doesn't overwrite the newest autosave; a defeat returns to the slot in play. A conversation that completes a quest or sets a chapter flag autosaves once it closes, in a safe place only, and counts as a real event for Continue's arrival rule. A damaged save falls back to its backup, a save with no parts comes back armed, and a save with missing lists loads with them empty. The save picker opens on the newest save, and replay routes pick rows by name (`pickRow`).

- **D205 - What a hero's actions cost (November 6).** Charge is paid by how much of a hit landed (`HitCharge` 4, `KillCharge` 6, `MultiHitCut` .6), so multi-hit skills don't pay for themselves on one target, and organs pay through the one route (`CombatActor.PayHit`). The swap's strike has a 4 s cooldown and no longer queues in a paused menu, after it out-broke every other source. Mashing dodge still evades, but earns no Flash and costs 30 Thrust (`MashCost`), with a steady tap at .45 s or slower free (`FlashRhythm`), and a .1 s beat between dodges (`DodgeGap`). A roll pressed while guarding evades. A guard still has no meter or facing rule: the rhythm gate is the smaller step, and whether tapped guards need more is **for Nathan's hands**. An earned crit belongs to the next hit that lands, not the next swing. Letting go of boost coasts, a spent boost rests to 30 as a sprint does, and a hull stores no speed into a wall; this is subject to Nathan flying it.

- **D206 - Boss length is measured on real chains, and the fight's numbers follow (November 6).** Boss length comes from what the replay party took (`tools/boss_durations.py`, 120 to 300 s), not from assumed uptime: Burrower 42000, Bellows 35000, Cantor 55000 (head 55%, links 15% each), Hessa 48000. On the last chain the driver took 126, 132 to 187, 148 and 145 s. **What a person takes still has to be timed on a pad.** Enemy damage in chapter 2 sits near 10% of a hero. Hessa's called crew hit at 1.2, on purpose lighter than the room's crew at 1.7, because they arrive in the middle of her fight and at 1.7 were the biggest thing hitting the party. Her chain has weight .5 at close range, so the due-move rule brings it round in every phase. A boss gets through everything it properly offers within a few picks, a phase shift brings its signature move next, and the Cantor's collar stalls after its song so the first stage has a punish.

- **D207 - Everything that strikes is marked, and a wall is a wall (November 6).** A marking reaches exactly as far as its hit and a shot stops at the end of its paint. Across time (bands, bubbles) only a hit that lands spends a cast's one hit; in one frame (toss, Rib Star) the marking does, so a perfect guard isn't hit twice at once. Every hit that isn't a boss marking stops at solid cover, whoever throws it: blades, bites, lunges, blasts and the swap's strike. `CombatCover` doesn't ask for a static flag, because built scenes don't store one. Enemies need a line of sight and walk on until they have one. Bosses' markings strike by shape, in open arenas. Only the Husk raises the Husk's shield.

- **D208 - The level curve keeps its shape (November 6).** 125 XP a level. The main path ends chapter 1 near Sync 7 and chapter 2 at Sync 8 to 10, 11 with every side quest, and Sync 12 stays out of reach until chapter 3. The plan's check on Charge at Sync 12 became what it was for: with Charge free, no skill beats its cooldown and a boss still lasts a minute at the driver's share. The Decks' low walls at 3 to 4.3 times their kit size are accepted, since nobody can see it on screen; the bar is 5, and the four bridge blocks at 15 to 18 times were rebuilt.

- **D209 - One rule for a press carried across a change of screen (November 6).** Whatever hands the world back through `GameInput.Blocked` holds Interact .35 s, so a thumb mashed through a load can't bounce the party back out of the exit it arrived beside. A screen that opens on a row that can spend something holds its first press .3 s. Only the button held at the moment a menu closed waits for its own release, so closing a menu can't deafen the next press. B steps back one level at a time. Salvage and selling a good part ask twice.

- **D210 - Hessa yields, and people's lines stay theirs (November 6).** She is the first person the party fights, so she stops rather than falls at zero. The person she is now takes over in the same frame and spot and says her piece unasked; the lift waits for it and hearing it saves. Keepers stand over her only once Aumi has been told, because it's Taren's word that sends them. Pell goes to Tallow when Abri says she has, and is never in both places. `BossWreck` keeps a fallen machine where it fell, over a flag gate, and pick-ups wait on their `visual`. Four of Abri's and Pell's beats that stay true for the rest of a save aren't marked as news. Orrin hails at the outer warp with a bark of his own, and **Nathan should approve Orrin's bark and the `teach.swap` lines.**

- **D211 - The game's own type and air (November 6).** Headings are in Cinzel and body text in Nunito, both open licences, under D001. Both use the full SDF shader, since the mobile shader ignores outlines. Loose HUD words share one per-font material with an underlay, and a material asset in Resources exists only to keep that shader variant in builds. Signs get one style at zone load (`SignStyle`), sized against the zone's camera, level to its yaw and settled on the real ground; builders only say what a sign says and where. Each natural place has its own ambience instead of a server-room hum. The HUD scales with Expand so it holds its corners on any screen.

- **D212 - The fixed camera moves to keep a fight in frame, never to a new angle (November 6).** Ground fights and conversations move the camera's look-at and distance through one solver, which holds the hero and the subject in their own parts of the screen and moves only when someone would leave them, up to 36 m, then an edge mark. It may slide inside 6 m, to 4 m at the least, only where it still can't see the hero. Under the lens a body goes whole with its shadow; between camera and hero it opens a hero-sized hole and keeps both. The objective's direction is an arrow on the panel, on screen, and never a word: with a fixed camera that's what the player can act on, and "behind" read as wrong when flying straight at the target. Barks live in a side column and panels fade for what's behind them, rather than the HUD being laid out around a play area the camera doesn't keep.

- **D213 - How the world is lit and stood up (November 6).** A marking is a faint wash and hatch under a strong rim and keyline, and its rim shows through what stands in it. One tinted below full alpha is a boundary, like a relay's ward, and gets neither keyline nor through pass. A boss's fall clears its markings, crew, shots and the party's lingering fields on that frame. Additional lights are smooth, not ramped, because the toon ramp's first band drew a hard rim on every pool of light; the sun keeps its bands. Toon floors under a low sun are lit as level below a slope limit. Underground is a faint cold fill too weak to cast a readable shadow, with light from fixtures you can see. The Gullet's shell is one material with tint and glow in vertex colour. Generated scatter drops, never moves, what it must keep off, because routes walk straight lines through what the RNG laid down. Things a word names are staged as that word says: a gate has a gate, a lift has a lift. An intake pitch on an already-upright model lays it flat and scales it by the wrong axis, so a prefab's bounds are checked against its fit axis. The Cantor's moving chain is 2.0 m a link, and resting stays 2.2.

- **D214 - A figure on the floor matches the face in the box (November 7).** A retexture can't fix a shape: Hessa was a headless armoured mech under a horned face, Brekk an ordinary man under a stone one, and Corra, Ishen and Pell wore someone else's outline. All five now have their own Meshy bodies, made the C8 way from their portraits (`tools/meshy/qa_body_batch.py`, 32 credits each), on the ids the game already used. Hessa keeps the Husk's lifecycle on her own skeleton. She yields from the first buckle of her fall with a slump laid over it, knees bent, head down, both feet planted (`GeneratedAnimator.YieldPhase`); the getting-up clip's lower pose read as a step up an invisible stair once a talk turned her side-on. `tools/blender/clip_poses.py` is how frames were picked, since no clip has a kneel of its own. The Burrower keeps its shape under a coat of plain cold steel: its first new coat asked for hazard stripes on the jaws, which Meshy painted as gold with black leopard blotches, an animal more than a drill, so the second asks for no yellow, orange, stripes or spots (`tools/meshy/qa_cast_batch.py`), and its material leans blue so it reads as steel under Sorrel's orange sun. Muster Skiffs wear hard red with hull lines. **Whether these read right in motion is for Nathan's eyes.**

- **D215 - A panel's plate steps aside, and its words never do (November 7).** A panel's plate fades only for what's genuinely behind it: the active hero, the target, the boss, an awake enemy, and the partner in a fight. Its words and bars stay solid in the heavier style, and the boss bar's fill keeps its colour. Words in the world go while they're cut by a fixed plate or the screen's edge and while a conversation or menu is up. Pause and shop are fully opaque, and the dialogue box has a solid backing, because the sheet's 254 of 255 opacity shows through in linear blending. The HUD reads the frame in LateUpdate, after the camera and every blow. The objective arrow has one sharp end, since the old triangle read as aimed elsewhere when turned. The boss card holds about 1.5 s with no band over the boss, and the Cantor's bar marks every link's share as well as the head's phases.

- **D216 - Who the camera and the door look after (November 7).** The hero shows through a boss in his own colours, with no tinted copy. The door opens only when the body's real surface stands between the lens and his chest: cells from a rigid body's mesh, the hurtbox for a rigged one. A ground fight frames hero and current target first, then the boss if it fits, and a ground shooter firing at the hero is framed up to 28 m. A rim moves the lens in a framed fight only if it really hides him, and only as far as it takes. Flight's pull-back and the view's middle ease in together as an encounter starts. Nothing over 3 m tall and over 7 m across the screen may cross the camera's line to a fight's floor or the walked path, and a kit piece fitted by height is seated by its footprint.

- **D217 - What colour says, and who shows in the dark (November 7).** Orange, red and purple on the floor mean danger; the party's rings are pale cream and cyan. A marking follows ground up to a rise of .7 per metre and stops at a wall's foot. Its hatch is soft, slants across lanes and on screen, and thins with the area. A spent marking clears in real time, and its outline shows through scenery but never across a body. A body that must read in the dark has a rim in shade and a least light (`_RimUnlit`, `_LightFloor`); daylight doesn't change. Lit things in a zone past Forward's six lights a renderer are fixtures and painted pools, not more lights.

- **D218 - People at rest (November 7).** Shaped heroes hold a guard stance only near a fight. Out of one they stand in a relaxed idle, and at rest people's arms hang. A partner's mark in a conversation is measured along the camera's axes, not the hero's, so she stands clear on screen, and talks that start on their own bring her over too. Lanternwick has ten unnamed townsfolk and two more stalls; they have no prompts, and the fallback if frame time moves is the first six. **Whether they read as a town is for Nathan's eyes.**

- **D219 - The Reach's sky and its curtain (November 7).** A flight camera doesn't rotate, so a skybox is fixed to the screen. The Reach's sky (`Lattice/DriftingSky`, `SkyDrift`) takes a turn from the view's travel over the flight plane, never from the lens's distance or, in the tilt, the clock. A line takes colour only at its points, so a fade along a two-point strand belongs in the texture (`curtain_soft.png`).

- **D220 - What the final audit changed (November 7).** Waypoint Blink follows the stick unless the stick points within 60 degrees of the target; then it goes straight away from the target, on foot and in flight, since thrust and stride are the same control and closing on something always holds that way. A body dragged more than .5 m in its attack loses it, tell or lunge, a body being dragged starts no tell, and a bite always lands from where it marked. A lunge that a wall turns off its lane stops there. A phase shift's brace runs on the shift's own clock, so a Flash can't end it early and a break can't cost it its move or its summons, and the Bellows braces by drawing down and spreading, having no rig to pose. Affixes reach both attacks as D008 says, though a weapon's Output stays with its own attack, so a Resonant Emitter's +4 now counts. The hand-back hold counts from the last press it eats, so A at a prompt answers only once the tapping pauses. Swap on the Skills page shows the other hero and swaps nothing; Gear's Swap and the Party row are the real ones. Upgrade rows give now and next on one line. Old saves are repaired on load only for what can be proved lost: Neve's cache, Hal's starting materials (the gift and its flag are consecutive in every revision of his first meeting) and Pell's move to Tallow. The title's logo is a designed wordmark, Meshy text-to-image, 9 credits (`Resources/UI/Title/wordmark.png`).

- **D221 - What the third look changed (November 7).** Menus show the world dimmed at .8 behind a solid framed plate, since signs and names now leave for a menu themselves and flat black read as a debug list; a shop shows its seller, the speaker of the conversation that opened it. The objective's arrow is convex with no notch, which at 20 px read as a V, a Y or a mouse pointer. The objective's frame is as tall as its words. The boss card stands just over the bar, unless the camera is about to carry the hero there. A word in the world goes in .1 s, for where it's heading as well as where it is, and leaves headroom over a prompt; in flight it holds its size as the camera draws back. A bark's plate slides along its column off a hero or a prompt's thing. With no fight on and a hero down, getting them up is the objective, by name (**Nathan should approve "Get Taren back up"**). A ship moves a plate by its hull. A boss's door dissolves in a fine grain with no wash. The Bellows' bubbles are purple and gather over the hero. **While an organ pumps, a breath marks that organ's half and nothing else, which changes the fight;** the ring round the dome is for when no organ is left. A mark past the size of the screen is a rim and a faint wash. A lane's paint ends in a rim at the last floor it has, and it still strikes on its plan. The ground camera holds the hero, what he's after and the way out of a marking, then the boss, then the partner if she fits, and any fight with something awake within 15 m is a framed fight. In flight the view's pull tops out at 50 m/s. A door in scenery opens for a real line to the chest. A point light's fixture may stand by a wall, its light may not. The replay route buys twelve gels at Corra's stall, because longer boss fights had spent the rest; the driver still wipes on Hessa about one attempt in two (204 and 723 damage taken across two attempts at the same numbers), which **a person has to judge on a pad.**

- **D222 - What the fourth look changed (November 7).** The fourth review found no Major, and this is the last batch before the build goes to Nathan, so only small, low-risk changes were taken. A consumable trains no ladder, only a part does: a recipe for one names no discipline, the field's default is Emitters, and twelve Repair Gels had taken Emitters from 2 to 6. A shop a conversation opens lands on its page toggle, which spends nothing, with the first offer one press down: a thumb still tapping through Corra's lines bought a 240-scrip part, and a longer hold can't help, because the next tap always lands after it. That takes back ui237's focus, since a stray press now only turns the page. The flight target's plate stays at the top of the screen and fades for what's behind it, as every plate does: batch 12 stepped it down into the playfield between the ship and what it was shooting, which was worse than what it fixed. The way's edge mark has a dark edge so it holds on a bright planet; the boss's mark keeps its look. A shot's drawn tail is at most 1.5 m, a comet's, where 2.9 m at the party's 24 m/s read as a beam when two shots lined up. While an organ pumps, the boss bar says OPENING only to a hero whose own target is the boss. Canyon walls cast shadow from their lit side again: two-sided, a wall with no lit side on the mesh laid a shadow nobody placed over the gorge's arrival, and the shadow pass opened it into rows of pale dashes. That puts back the old short row of E3-lanternwick-08, and the real cure, one welded shadow caster, isn't small. A marking under alpha .99 is a boundary, not a danger: a soft line near its outer edge that falls away inward, with no rim, hatch or keyline, and a relay's ward is lamp-coloured at .34. A column of light is drawn on `curtain_soft`, since the particle shader ignores tiling and the flare on a line is one spark halfway up: the bays' and the survey key's light had only ever been a tiny cyan tick. A floor sign in a flight zone grows as the camera draws back, so SALVAGE EDDY lies near the tube's middle, where the bed is level. The Great Lantern's seal is the lamps' colour, well under a curtain's strength, not Hessa's orange. The gorge's scatter has no `CrystalClusterA`, whose glass is a flat teal fill. Rubble by the wrecks is burned. The Halo's and Tallow Approach's skies take D219's drifting sky, which draws exactly as before in a ground zone that shares the material. A check that puts the party somewhere in one frame judges the camera once it has settled, since the flight view comes in at D221's pace after the jump. **Not taken, for Nathan:** the Bellows' membrane seal has no frame (it does go when the boss falls); Sela's shots at targets off screen; Hal's bench opening on a craft row (it spends 2 Scrap Alloy); a cap on how far a lying sign grows.
- **D223 - The shipping scope: ten chapters, every requested region, each made compact (November 11).** Nathan asked for the game to be finished without check-ins, with only new music left over. The atlas's 52 maps and 40-46 hours can't be built at the quality the QA pass set, and cutting whole regions would drop places Nathan asked for on September 28 and wrote music for. So every requested region stays, as one compact chapter each: a space approach with its own rule, a settlement, one combat map or dungeon, one boss. Veyr, Merrow, Pale Exchange, the optional destinations and a fourth hero are cut, and their story jobs move: Idra (now Idra Sorn, which settles scri108 in Pell's favour) joins at Hoarfell in a Rauk cutter frame, Ilo carries the omitted-households thread, Taren's route lock moves to the Picket, and Nacre Grave opens chapter 9. That is 30 new scenes and 13 boss fights in story order, each chapter finished before the next; if the run stops early, chapters 9 and 10 are never the ones cut. A systems pass comes first (impact, loot, skills and EXTRA moves, a route chart, town life, the third hero). Meshy has 2,372 credits for all of it, about 280 a chapter, so kits lean on procedural geometry, shared pieces and retextures. The plan is [SHIPPING_PLAN.md](SHIPPING_PLAN.md); each chapter's brief is under `docs/chapters/`.

- **D224 - A guard pays for what it stops (November 10).** Holding guard took 65% off any hit for free, so a boss's special could be answered by standing still. This was boss269's design call, handed over when Nathan asked for every open issue to be addressed. Each guarded hit now spends Thrust in proportion to what it stopped: 250 for a whole Integrity bar, measured after plating, so a heavier frame makes a guard cheaper as well as a hit lighter. A read combo of three or four ordinary blows costs about half the bar, and two of a boss's big hits in a row nearly all of it. Thrust comes back at 6 a second under a guard instead of 17, or a guard held through everything would pay for itself. With too little Thrust left the guard breaks: the hit lands whole, the bar empties and flashes as a refused roll's does, the hero staggers .6 s, and X must come up before the guard rises again. A Flash Guard is never charged. For a player: guard a combo you've read, dodge a boss's big hits. No chapter route holds guard and the partner never guards; the old `c0-down-revive` route does, and its guard will now break. **Nathan should judge the rate on a pad.**

- **D225 - A boss's trophy makes a part (November 10).** Husk Core, Choir Resin and Cantor Pearl were "keep this: no use yet" (prog158). Each is now the key material of one part a little past the Refined part in its slot, at a rank anyone has, so it's worth making the day it drops, at Hal's bench or any Lattice Anvil: Husk Frame (2 Husk Core and 4 Scrap Alloy; Plating 20 against 16), Choir Emitter (4 Choir Resin and 6 Scrap Alloy; Output 25 with Resonance 4) and Pearl Module (the Cantor Pearl and 6 Scrap Alloy; Resonance 14 and Fortune 8 against 10 and 6). They're parts, not consumables, because Mira's stall sells every consumable in the catalogue, which would have sold the trophy's use for 20 scrip. None is on sale, salvage gives no trophy back, and no scrip was added, so prog159 and prog160 stand as they were. **Not right in `ff99a7a`:** the Choir Emitter's Resonance never reaches a fight there. An Emitter's own stats count only for its own attack and Resonance is read from the Edge's sum (`Gear.Calculate`, `PartyController.RefreshStats`), so in combat it equals a Refined Emitter, and the check compares stat sums and can't see it.

- **D226 - A plate that would lie on someone slides along its own edge, and words left on a faded plate keep a backing (November 10).** Plates only faded (D215, D222), which left their words printed on whoever stood behind. The target card slides along the top between the vitals and the objective, and with no room for the whole card it keeps its first line against the top edge; the prompt bar slides along the foot off the party and the thing it names; the bark column keeps off awake enemies and the lanes they mark. Nothing steps into the playfield, which D222 took back. As a plate steps aside its words get a soft band hugging their letters, and the skill row's labels always have one. The boss state keeps 26 units off the skill row and shrinks to no less than 28.5. The HUD publishes its plates' screen rects each frame (`HudKeepOut`) for the partner to keep out from under.

- **D227 - The partner fires where she points, at what the player can see, from where the player can see her (November 10).** A shot leaves along the heading unless the target is inside the aim's cone, so running out of a marking or round the lead she fired along her run, away from the boss and off the screen. She now fires only within 20 degrees (flight) or 35 (on foot) of her target, and turns to face it once stopped. She takes the lead's target while its middle is on screen; off it, the boss if it shows, else the nearest enemy on screen, else she holds fire, and nothing is fired while she herself is off screen. Where she comes to rest is kept whole on screen and out from under the HUD's plates (`HudKeepOut`): the nearest place that fits, within her reach of a visible target or near the lead, on open floor, out of boss markings. She may fire less, so boss lengths (D206) are to be re-measured.

- **D228 - A marking is one closed shape (November 10).** A marking's area and its outline draw separately from one mesh (`TelegraphFill` at Transparent-11, `Telegraph` at -10). Areas lay once per pixel and outlines don't cross another marking's inside, so markings that land together read as one shape with one edge; drawn in one pass, three lanes stacked their hatch into a lattice with bright bars across the hero. Where the ground cuts a marking (D217) the paint stops and the rim closes along the cut, on floor the player can see. A lane is closed at its origin too. The charge glows behind its front instead of a bright band, which read as a rung on a narrow lane and as a column under a boss. The rim is thinner, and never under about three pixels wherever the camera stands. A shot's marking stays charged ahead of the shot until it lands (`Telegraph.Carry`): an enemy's ranged shots and volleys, the Bellows' comets among them, and Hessa's sidearm, whose lanes used to go as the shot left. The party's rings follow the ground as markings do. The Cantor's song is red, since purple disappears on the Gullet's floor.

- **D229 - The flight view holds back, a lying sign keeps to its floor, and the curtain keeps time with Sela (November 10).** In a fight the flight view holds as far back as it last needed for 1.6 s and then comes in at no more than 20 m/s: coming in at 28 the moment the bounds shrank, it pumped out and in three times a cluster in the Gullet. The pull-out stays at D221's 50 m/s for the Cantor's sake. A lying sign in flight grows with the camera only as far as its letters stay over the floor, measured once, which is the cap D222 left open. A floor sign thins to a quarter while a hero stands between it and the camera over its letters, but not while one stands on its line: LOWER CHAMBER was a floor sign all along, and legs through its middle read 'LO R CHAMBER'. The Wick curtain holds each strand near full for a third of its 1.9 s and parts outward over 2.3 s, framed from its middle to the ship, so it comes down while Sela says so.

- **D230 - Saves write behind the frame, menus warm at the title, and the game quits in order (November 10).** A save is made into text on its frame and written by one ordered worker in a player; any read of a slot and quitting wait for queued writes. The editor writes on the frame, because its suites read and delete save files around every save. The catalog keeps each kind of definition, read once from its own folder. The title warms the menus' fonts, sheets and dates, one thing a frame. Each of these had cost a 34 to 46 ms frame at a menu or a save in the final chain (QA-034). The bench opens on a recipe filter that spends nothing, with the first recipe one press down, as D222's shops do (QA-036). Replay routes name the bench row they want (`pickRow`, until `rowReached`) and no longer count presses, since the filter row and the trophies' parts moved every position. The replay and the Quit buttons go out through `OrderlyQuit`, which lets the device retire its last work first; it eases the engine's crash at shutdown (QA-031), and doesn't cure it. If the crash recurs, the next Unity 6000.4 patch is the thing to try.

- **D231 - What the last open looks changed in the world (November 10).** The world half of the open Minors is one isolated builder, `QaNov10Staging.Apply` (marker `QA_NOV10_STAGED_OK`; `-qa-zones` limits it). It runs after `ChapterOneStaging`, and has to be run again whenever chapter 1 is restaged or Hushwell, the Gullet or Sorrel is rebuilt. What it adds is drawn only. The Bellows' doors are the width of the way they close and 2.6 m tall, tucked into stone posts over a low sill: a bare 15 by 6 m slab of membrane read as a sheet propped at the chamber's edge. The Gullet's arrival has a lower lip behind the ships, rising to tall folds at the walls, with pale teeth along it, and it stays under the flight plane over the lane so nothing stands between the camera and a ship. Tallow's hull keeps its 1024 paint and gets crisp plating over it in world space (LatticeToon's `_PANELS_ON`, about four metres a tile), because a larger copy of a smeared paint would only be sharper smudges. Sorrel's slope gets cut edges and a ledge line on the ground's own strata, on steep faces only (`_StrataSlope.w`); kit strata laid on it read as the curved brick walls an earlier review threw out. The site's rig is canted hard toward the bore and sooted, with torn plates, scorch and smoke, and the Burrower's wreck smokes too. The first job's gate is a leaf of steel posts, amber rails and a striped kick plate, and its hatch has a raised striped collar whose lit inside face shows the hole goes down (`MarketOpeningStaging`). A canyon landform's shadow comes from one welded caster, the collision heightfield drawn as shadows only, and the drawn floor and walls only receive, which is the cure D222 named for the gorge's dashes. Canyon landforms are built on half-metre cells, which halves the teeth where floor meets wall and doesn't end them. **None of this has been seen in a capture.**
- **D232 - A hit has a weight, and only real attacks carry one (November 11).** Combat had no hit-stop, a hero's hit never touched the camera, ten of fourteen enemy bodies didn't react to being struck, and taking damage looked the same as dealing it. `DamagePacket` now carries a `HitWeight` (Tick, Light, Medium, Heavy, Finisher); a hero's packet with none given goes by its break power, so a skill written later lands as hard as it breaks. A packet built by hand weighs nothing: tests and fixtures build hundreds, and a default that did anything would hold the clock under all of them. Every number is in one table in `Impact.cs`.
- **D233 - A hit-stop holds both clocks, and has rules (November 11).** Heroes run on real time and the world on scaled time, so a time-scale-only stop froze the enemy while the hero swung on (D056). `GameTime.Stop` holds both. Two stops are the longer, never the sum; none exceeds 250 ms; a pause ends one; a Flash's slow motion returns after one. Only the held hero's hits, dealt or taken, reach the clock, the camera, the accents and the pad, as with a Flash. Stops keep to 15% of any two seconds, one beat per swing, halved inside a Flash; a boss going down and the held hero falling are held regardless. The replay driver keeps its pad through a held frame.
- **D234 - The camera kicks in its own plane; bodies flash, give and are shoved (November 11).** The kick is translation in the camera's plane, sized as a share of the view's height so ground and flight read the same, with `Tremor` folded in (D212 forbids a new angle). A struck body flashes through a shader property read before it is written, since a boss's see-through door and the Burrower's lamps write the same renderers. Only unrigged bodies are pressed and set back; the four rigged fighters keep their authored recoil (D095). A blow carries an awake pack hunter or swarm body .35 m (a finisher .8 m), never one telling or making an attack and never a sleeping one, so a combo cannot cancel what a body has marked. This was the lead's call under D223, not left for Nathan.
- **D235 - Accents, the hurt edge, and the player's own dials (November 11).** Nineteen new clips from the Kenney packs already owned layer over the existing impacts under new keys and draw no random numbers. A hit on the held hero reddens the screen's edge under the HUD and his sparks are red-orange, not the cyan of his own hits; the low-integrity pulse runs only with something awake nearby. Shake (Full, Reduced, Off), hit pause, flashes and rumble are settings on the pause menu and the title. Rumble goes only to a pad that reports XInput.
- **D236 - Gear carries effects, not only stats (November 11).** Parts fed a fight five flat numbers. `Mods` (32 additive fields) now rides on affixes, parts and consumables; gear bonuses add to each other, then multiply weakness, Break and crit. Twenty affixes exist, their stats scaling with the part's tier and their effects not; world parts roll none, one or two (55/35/10), shop parts come clean, an overclock picks at random from the pool. Fourteen modules each change how a hero plays (Glass Lattice, Twin Thread, Reservoir, Second Wind and the rest), and four Type Prisms lift every hit of their type by 15%. Effect text is generated from the numbers, so a row cannot promise what the part doesn't do. Five tiers: Scrap, Refined, Prime, Tempered, Choirwrought; nothing reaches tier 4 or 5 until its chapter places it.
- **D237 - Pouches have caps, benches have prices, stalls have sellers (November 11).** This settles prog159 and prog160. Consumables have carry caps (Repair Gel 9, Charge Cell 5) and heal a share of the bar; loot over a cap is paid in scrip, and an old save over a cap is paid down once on load. A shared 4 s wait binds between items in a fight only, and no consumable is fabricated while a fight is on. Upgrades are bench only and cost Scrap, scrip and, from Prime, the region's materials; salvage refunds half; re-tune rerolls one affix. Shops buy materials. A stall is found by zone and speaker (`Rootmarket_Binna`), each offer with its own price. A recipe stays off the bench until its later-chapter ingredients have been held once.
- **D238 - Chapter content is registered from definitions, and what is missing fails by name (November 11).** Adding a zone, an enemy or a boss meant hand-editing id-keyed switches in a dozen files and twenty lists in tests, several of which failed silently (a zone with no music, a boss fighting with another boss's moves, a zone no test visited). Music, scene registration, look specs, display names, the objective line, boss kits and the tests' lists now come from `ZoneDef`, `EnemyDef`, `QuestDef` and each region's own files. Chapters 1 and 2 keep their hand-written entries, frozen. An unregistered boss throws in the editor; a zone with no look spec cannot be saved; `ContentScaffold.Check` lists what a chapter still lacks. The `bridge` ban applies to the nine chapter 1-2 scripts only (it was the Decks walkway's retired name), and the two ten-quest pins name the ten by id.
- **D239 - The new peoples (November 11).** Each new region gets its own people, so no town looks like another Vael town, and each has one expression instrument for its portraits as plumes are for the Vael: the **Okhari** of Xylos (short, broad river folk; gill-frills), the **Ilu** of Driftreed (tall wading folk; feathered antennae and glow-spots, who speak across water in light), the **Rauk** of Veyr (carved-stone mountain folk; brow plates and a crest of horn slabs). The first Rauk sheet read as a grey human man with braids and was rejected; the second is Idra. Hadda, not Wen, is Rootmarket's mender, because chapter 4 has Maru Wend.
- **D240 - Scripts wait in `docs/writing/pending/` until their chapter is defined (November 11).** Builders copy every `.yarn` directly under `docs/writing/` into the game, and the dialogue tests then demand a portrait set per speaker and a `QuestDef` per quest. Chapter 4's and later scripts are written ahead of their chapters and held in the subfolder, which the copy doesn't read.
- **D241 - One tree, many hands: runs wait instead of failing (November 11).** Up to nine engineers edit one working tree and share one Unity. `scripts/exec-when-green.ps1` (an editor method), `scripts/focused.ps1` (compile, EditMode, named PlayMode classes) and `scripts/suite-shared.ps1` (the whole suite in eight parts) each wait for a tree that compiles and a Unity that is free, and try again when someone's half-finished edit breaks the compile under them. The Meshy bridge locks its manifest. Engineers change shared types in a compile-preserving order.
- **D242 - Three more skills a hero, and an EXTRA on its own gauge (November 11).** Nothing was learned after Sync 12 of 40. Taren learns Bulwark Rush (16), Cinder Brand (20) and Severance (24); Sela learns Mend Lattice (16, the game's first heal that isn't an item, which is what makes a gel cap fair), Prism Relay (20) and Long Exposure (24). Each hero has an EXTRA (Anvil Chorus, Lattice Aria) on a second gauge that fills from damage dealt and taken and from Flashes, never decays and is saved; it is fired with RB+LT behind a half-second cut-in of the Shaped portrait, with no camera change. The gauge doesn't exist before `extra.unlocked`, which chapter 3's first scene sets. One EXTRA crosses at most one boss phase line. The partner never fires one. An EXTRA is a `SkillDef` on `CharacterDef.extra`, never in a slot.
- **D243 - Sela's chart is a page, and the way back to any town (November 11).** From `xylos.charted` the pause menu has a Chart page: stops as lantern points on a painted chart, the current place and the main objective's stop marked, travel to any stop the party has reached. Chapters 1 and 2's four stops are charted by their `met.` flags, later ones by `visited.<Zone>`. Travel is refused, with the reason, in a conversation, in a boss fight, within 15 m of an awake enemy (the repair cradle's radius, not the warp's 6 m, so the chart is no escape from a fight a few steps off) and mid-encounter in flight. Stops are `ChartStop` definitions each region's builder adds.
- **D244 - Three difficulties, described by their numbers (November 11).** Gentle (enemies deal 60% and have 80% integrity), Standard (the game as tuned) and Demanding (130% and 115%). Asked once at New Game in a real player, changeable in Settings at any time, stored in the save, with no mark on the HUD and nothing else changed. Damage is scaled where an enemy's hit lands, so shots in the air, summons and specials are all reached; integrity is fixed when a body is made. Tests and the replay driver run Standard unless a route names another.
- **D245 - Every chapter opens and closes with its card (November 11).** The chapter-complete card was hard-coded for chapter 2. From chapter 3 a main quest's `chapter`, title, `cardFlag` and `cardBody` drive a `CHAPTER n` card as its quest begins and a `CHAPTER n COMPLETE` card when its flag turns true in play, never on load. Chapters 1 and 2 keep theirs as they are.
- **D246 - Ground is painted, cut and scattered by a kit, and measured (November 11).** A fixed three-quarter camera shows mostly floor, and outside the Decks most floors were one flat tone: `tools/look_density.py` measures the largest stretch of plain floor in a still (the Decks 0.05 to 0.21; the canyon 0.34 to 0.61; Hushwell up to 0.90), and 0.22 is the limit for every play-zoom still from here on. `GroundKit` gives a terrain three painted material layers through a splat mode in the toon shader, a flat layer of batched decals (paving, cracks, drifts, pools), low cover that never collides, lamp pools and slow moving shade, with every texture painted locally in Python and none bought. Lantern Gorge, Lanternwick and Sorrel are retrofitted (the canyon now 0.05 to 0.20, Lanternwick 0.01 to 0.14), the canyon's floor and rock are cut along the line where the height itself turns steep rather than along cells (which ends the cliff-foot teeth and took its mesh from 61 MB to 12), and the gorge's road is worn a few centimetres into its collision. `GroundStaging.Apply` runs last in any rebuild.
- **D247 - Tests run in mirrors of the project, not in it (November 12).** With several engineers sharing one working tree and one Unity, fourteen test and build runs were queued behind the single editor and some waited eighty minutes; two PlayMode runs that started in the same second also stopped each other, because each save shield found the other's stash of the human saves. A test mirror is a second copy of the project beside the repository (`..\CoronachTest\A`, `C`, and `S` for the whole suite) with its own Unity Library, made and refreshed by `scripts/mirror-sync.ps1` (about 6 GB, under two minutes; later copies take seconds). `focused.ps1` copies the tree to a free mirror once it compiles, waiting out a builder that is still writing scenes, and runs there; `suite-shared.ps1` copies once and runs every part on that frozen copy, so a suite's verdict is for one state of the tree. A mirror keeps its saves in its own folder through `-savepath` and has no shield, so it cannot touch the human saves. Builders, renders, intake and anything with a player window stay in the main project, one at a time behind the `Global\CoronachUnityTurn` lock. Each Unity takes 2 to 3 GB; four at once is this machine's ceiling, and a test that is sensitive to load is rerun alone before it is called a failure.
- **D248 - A card for what something paid waits behind the card that paid it (November 12).** A quest reward that overflowed a carry cap showed POUCH FULL before QUEST COMPLETE, because every posted card was treated as the answer to a press and went to the front. Cards now post one of two ways: `Notices.Post` for the answer to a press (SAVED, CACHE OPENED, SECOND WIND), which goes next and cuts the card on screen to 0.4 s, and `Notices.Pay` for what something paid (NEW PART from a drop, cache or reward; POUCH FULL for loot over a cap), which waits behind the quest or kill card that paid it and the level that came with it. A quest reward reads QUEST COMPLETE, SYNC, POUCH FULL; a kill reads QUEST UPDATED, SYNC, POUCH FULL, NEW PART.
- **D249 - An enemy's behaviour is data beside its archetype, and chapter 3 sets the rules later enemies follow (November 12).** `EnemyDef.behaviour` picks one of eight families (Charger, Lobber, Ambusher, SwarmCloud, Guardian, Anchored, Tetherer, Strafer) and `tune` and `attacks[]` shape it; `archetype` still says what kind of body it is to hooks, shoves and the camera, and `None` fights as before. The rules that came with them: a lingering area is a boundary unless it damages, and is nobody's attack; a hide is ringed only while occupied, and an ambusher hides only once awake; a latch or tether never damages after the catch; a swarm is one body drawn as motes; a slowed hull's top speed follows `Health.Pace`. For bosses: break targets carry shares of the boss's bar and cannot be hit before the fight starts, a boss that survives leaves through a placed `BossExit` (`Ending.Leaves`), and a mini-boss states its own duration on its sheet (amends D206's two to five minutes, which stays the rule for a chapter's boss). From chapter 3 on, attacks are authored at their real weight and a zone's `enemyThreat` is 1 unless the zone is meant to be a little harder (the Walking Canopy is 1.1); chapters 1 and 2 keep their rising dial (D194).
- **D250 - Wendmire: the Veil's rule is drawn by the world, the tide moves walks not floors, and each chapter's offer waits behind the last one's afterword (November 12).** Chapter 4's flight zone makes "visibility is the resource" playable without words: the combat side's `ShipGlow` lights a ship as it fires, boosts and uses skills; `S/World/GlowField.cs` wakes each of the swarms hung asleep and on show in the lane the moment a lit ship comes within 30 m (a dark one has to fly into a cloud to wake it); `S/UI/GlowGauge.cs` puts the glow under the vitals (LIT, DARK) only where a ship has one. Driftreed's tide (`S/World/TideSwing.cs`) swings two rope walks away and two across: each walk's ends are `Membrane` seals (a rope, a box, a carving obstacle) so the bake can lay floor under every walk and the shared seal-opening tests apply; the bake opens every seal while it runs, because a shut seal's obstacle cut the editor's own path check. Wendmire's lamps speak in counts (`S/World/LampRhythm.cs`: two short one long, long long short, short long short, a lure's held-nothing flicker). And, departing from the chapter 4 manifest's "takes the place of `AbriDinnerServed`": Abri's chapter 4 offer waits until her chapter 3 afterword has been heard (`SideStories.ChapterThreeAfterword`, as chapter 3's offer waits behind chapter 2's), so each chapter still ends on its own last word.
- **D251 - The Veil's light is the lead ship's, the field spans the lane, and a tide walk's open end means the walk is there (November 12).** Two holes in D250, found by checking the combat side's `ShipGlow` rates against the lane: six swarms hung within 28 m of a 144 m lane's middle, so a ship flown lit along either edge passed more than 30 m from all of them, and `GlowField` counted every hero ship, so the partner's own catch-up boost (PartnerBrain boosts whenever it is ten metres behind, and a boost lights a hull) woke the field under a player who had flown it dark. The field is now three rows of three across the lane (48 m apart, the outermost 20 to 28 m in from the edge: no line across a row is out of a lit ship's reach, and between two swarms there are 36 m to fly dark), and only the ship the player flies draws the swarms; `WendmireWorldTests` flies the dark way with the partner under her own brain. The rates themselves stand. And `TideSwing` keeps "an end is open only where the walk is": anything else that opens an end (the world tests' open-every-seal) brings the walk across with it, where before a body walked out of an open end onto water and stuck. Chapter 4's last pump reports `LastPump`, not `Pump`: one report on two steps let the first pumps' work do the last's (QaPlanWorldTests.NoQuestMovesOnOutOfTurn).
- **D252 - Many Hands' hull: the frame holds on only at a join, the joins are coupling drums just under the plane, and a fight in a safe town is a room, not the zone (November 12).** Chapter 5's flight zone (`ManyHandsBuilder.Hull.cs`, docs/maps/ManyHandsHull.md) shows the city the party has just walked through from outside, every vessel in its own make and paint over roofs dressed by a seeded roofscape, and draws the fight's rule: the four joins Hulljack has filed on are raised coupling drums whose decks stand at y .3 (the combat engineer's contract: the frame stands on the plane, where its feet are hit, and a foot drawn on a roof six metres down would show five metres south of where it is struck), each with three pieces of the hull's own gear (a mast, a crane's boom, a cargo tower) solid at the plane between 16 and 26 m, which is the cover the torch, grapple and debris stop at. Everything drawn and not solid stays under the plane: a three-high container stack, a gantry and the bow's lantern tower that stood up through it were cut down, because a ship flies through anything drawn-only and reads it as cover it isn't. The limits are a harbour line of lamp buoys with a lit line strung at the ships' height, so a ship stops at a line it sees. And Hulljack's second part, fought on foot in the town's freight hall, makes only that room a combat place: `FightPocket` (`S/World/FightPocket.cs`, guide 15.11) flips the zone to `GroundCombat` while the lead stands in the hall between `bossdown.Hulljack` and `bossdown.DessikVarn` and switches its working, fighting and after dressing, and `ZoneController.InSafePocket` now means a non-combat kind that differs from the zone's, so an enemy in a fight pocket fights instead of going home to wait.
- **D253 - A test run keeps its key rebinds beside its own settings, never in the editor's shared preferences (November 12).** Three keyboard checks failed in the whole suite with no code change behind them. The Unity editor's preferences are one registry key for every copy of the project, the test mirrors included; one mirror's rebind test was half-way through a rebind when another mirror's fixture saved the bindings, and that fixture wrote the half-finished rebind back for every later run (attack H, dodge G, guard Y). `KeyBindingStore` now keeps rebinds in a file beside the run's own settings whenever a run is isolated (`-audio-settings-path` or `-savepath`, which every test run has), as sound levels and impact settings already were; a player still uses PlayerPrefs. The check that no surface names the other device's buttons no longer mistakes a keyboard key the player bound (Y) for the pad's Y. Anything else a test writes to shared preferences is a fault of the same kind.
- **D254 - A boss's one-line target card keeps its line (November 12, amends D226).** For a boss the target card is one line, the lock and how to switch targets. When the HUD found no clear seat in the top band it kept "only the first line" of a card, and treated a card with no line break as having none, so in most of a boss fight nothing on screen said how to get from the boss to what is around it. A one-line card now counts as its own first line: it moves up against the screen's top edge or stays and fades with its backing. Two-line cards are cut as before.
- **D255 - Idra Sorn's kit, and she joins on the same ladder as the others (November 12).** Taren cuts, Sela shoots from range, Idra plants her feet: the slowest and heaviest blows in the party (a three-hit hammer chain whose third knocks light bodies down), a Brace where the others roll (a held guard that stops 85 percent, with a shockwave on a Flash Guard), a short hop, and skills that keep her partner standing (Shelter, Rescue Flare, Hold the Line). Her late skills are Take the Weight at Sync 16 (for six seconds every hit that would land on her partner lands on her instead, through her Plating and Brace), Cutting Torch at 20 (a rooted burn that goes through a guardian's plated front) and Bulkhead at 24 (a six-second wall of light that stops enemy shots and holds ordinary bodies on their side; never a hero, a hero's shot, or a body already telling an attack), with the EXTRA move Last One Out. She joins at the party's Sync on the same unlock levels and mastery as Taren and Sela, so at Sync 16 she knows nine skills; the chapter 6 brief's "five skills known" is superseded, because a hero who arrives behind the ladder is a hero the player benches.
- **D256 - Chapter 6's main quest lets the Host Marshal's fight carry the couplings, and the Aurora Shear's rule is a current a ship rides (November 12).** The brief makes the three thaw couplings the Marshal's break targets, so `LongSleep` (HoarfellBuilder.Quests) drops the manifest's optional "Interact x3 Coupling" step, as section 4 of docs/writing/ch06-manifest.md allows, and its nine steps are the chart's lane, Idra's hail, Lastlight, Tern, the plan, the join at the bottom gallery (its arrow finds a point with report `BottomGallery`), `Kill HostMarshal`, the long sleep, and the lift figures aboard. In the Shear a current (`S/World/AuroraCurrent.cs`) carries every hero ship inside it at its push, fills Thrust for a ship flying within 70 degrees of its flow and holds one flying within 70 degrees of against it to .7 of its top speed while draining Thrust; what says so is the ribbon's chevrons and streaks running the way it flows and the HUD's current gauge (`S/UI/CurrentGauge.cs`), never words alone. The first current turns round once Idra has hailed, and her cutter tows the party across into her lane (`S/World/CutterTow.cs`), so the brief's "the current turns, she pulls them out" is played, not told.
- **D257 - How hard things hit and how long a boss lasts are measured on the party a player really has (November 12).** Chapter 3's enemies were authored for a hero on paper and landed at 5 to 9 percent of the bar; its bosses hit softer than the road to them; and the shared boss-length test measured every boss in chapter 2's loadout, understating the real party by about 40 percent from chapter 3 on. The rule from here: an ordinary hit takes 9 to 13 percent of a hero's bar at the Sync and in the parts a player has where that enemy is met, a boss's moves take 12 to 16 percent in both phases and never less than the heaviest ordinary hit on the road to it, and both are measured through `Health.Receive` by a test in the chapter's combat class. A boss from chapter 3 on is timed in its own chapter's `DevLoadout`: a chapter's boss lasts two to five minutes (about three is the aim) and a mini-boss states its own bounds on its sheet. As re-tuned: Seedthane 27,000 (68 to 80 s), Floodcrown 62,000 (about 170 to 185 s), Rotbloom 54,000 (about 150 to 180 s). Chapters 1 and 2's four bosses are measured as they always were and play as they did.
- **D258 - A weak spot sits where a blade can reach it, and every boss stands on its floor (November 12).** `BossController` placed weak spots within 1.2 m of a boss's centre, which is inside the body of anything as large as the Rotbloom, and two chapter 3 bosses floated because a capsule shorter than twice its radius is stretched by the engine. On a body wider than 1.4 m the three spots now sit just outside the capsule, ahead and on each flank, or where the boss's Kit says (`Kit.weakSpots`); narrower bodies, which is chapters 1 and 2's four, keep the old places exactly. Shared tests now require every boss to have a spot a hero can reach from the edge of its body (all of them for bosses after chapter 2), and every later boss fought on foot to rest within 15 cm of its floor. A cover pod that falls, or is broken, moves anyone under it clear before it turns solid.
- **D259 - Heroes climb upright, and Idra walks on her own stride and flies from her own engines (November 12).** Walking uphill, both heroes folded: the torso dived into the slope and the stepping knee came up beside the hip (`docs/validation/C2-uphill-fold.md`). The cause was the frame, not the numbers. `GroundFeet` tilted the whole leg chain back into the slope's frame, which left the hips behind and below both feet. Every repair of that (pitching the spine forward 1.9 times the slope, sliding the hips 65% back upright after the feet were placed, then dropping them up to 36 cm to keep the stretched trailing leg planted) folded the legs, and five retunes on November 4 only moved the fold. Walking, running and sprinting up a grade over about 10 degrees are now posed in the gravity frame (UprightClimb); running folded harder than walking (from 20 degrees the faster runs sank to 60 to 80% of their flat hip height with the stepping knee up to 38 cm above the hip). A run stands upright only as far as its hips rise at 2.5 m/s or less: fully upright, a 6.7 m/s run up 39 degrees lifts them 5.4 m/s and the stance knee straightened at 3,200 deg/s; capped, the runs hold 88 to 95% of their flat height at 39 degrees and the knee stays inside its continuity bounds. Hips and torso stand upright, and only the feet keep the slope's frame. Foot targets ride on the ground under them, and the step covers level distance, so a steep climb takes shorter steps. A climbing step holds its heel until its toe takes over where the rolled-down foot put it, and the trailing heel peels up about the planted toe (at most 60 degrees) instead of the hips dropping. Flat ground and downhill are unchanged in every gait. `UphillStepAudit`, run on the slopes `GroundSlopeSurvey` finds in the game (99th percentile 21 to 31 degrees, steepest 35) and at 39: the committed walk failed 36 of 36 cases from 15 degrees up; this passes 36 of 36, with swing knees 90 to 100 degrees and bob 5 to 10 cm, the flat walk's own range. Idra: her two ground bodies get stride profiles measured from their own soles (`StrideCalibration.BuildIdra`): walk, run and sprint read off each planted toe, calibrated to the convention Taren's and Sela's were stored in (their run and sprint read back within 0.7%, their walks sit 4.7% over the toe's reading). Her take pulls the foot away at the loose heel-to-toe handover, so her profiles hold it the way every climbing step does (`holdsHandover`); her flat landing otherwise straightened the knee at 1326 deg/s. IdraNatural is 1.01, 5.83 and 8.78 m/s; IdraBraced is 1.05, 6.02 and 9.04. Before this she had no profile, so `GroundFeet` never ran on her and her feet skated. Her cutter's engines are the two round wells in its stern deck (`FlightHullMeasure`).
