Each socket sits on its well's stern lip, over the plating, so the exhaust leaves the well in the open; the sockets read off by eye sat low against the wells' rear walls, where the flame ran into the hull. Her hull's furthest vertex is 2.161 m, inside the 2.17 m radius the party-of-three work gave her.
- **D260 - A party of three fields two, and the third rests (November 12).** Idra Sorn joins in chapter 6 and the party becomes three. Two are out on the field and the third rests with no body anywhere; the pair is stored in `GameState.deployed`, and with two heroes nothing changes. The pair is chosen on the Party page, in a safe zone or pocket or at a bench, and refused anywhere else with the reason. Swap moves only between the two out. Every kill and quest pays all three, so a resting hero never falls behind in Sync; mastery grows by use only. If both heroes out fall it is the defeat screen, nobody comes in from rest, and Retry brings back the same pair with everyone whole; "nobody standing", for autosaves and load repairs, means the two out. A resting hero speaks in conversations by portrait; a world bark meant for them is said by the lead in the lead's own words or not at all, and radio hails are exempt. The Skills page, and with three the Gear page, cycle through all three with Swap and change nobody on the field. Idra joins with four skills in her slots (Breach, Rescue Flare, Shelter, Slag Hammer) and one card, with the rest in the chooser. Her hull radius is 2.17 m, and ships arrive apart by their own hulls (4.15 m for Taren and Idra, still 3.7 m for Taren and Sela). The loadout `chapter6` is the arrival with two heroes; `chapter6joined` is after the join. An old two-hero save loads and plays as before.
- **D261 - The Orrery is one machine with one rule, and the lane can only be crossed through its gates (November 12).** Chapter 7's space approach (docs/maps/Orrery.md) is nine moons, each threaded on a light-rail that is a ring round Oriel, all on one twenty-second clock, neighbouring rails turning opposite ways. One rule, worked out every frame from where each moon is (`S/World/OrreryRail.cs`), drives everything a player reads: a rail's lamps stand amber over the stretch its moon is about to sweep and run green everywhere else; each rail has a row of three bronze rail-gates just outside it, and a gate is amber from the moment the moon's amber reach comes to it until the moon is a hull past it, green otherwise; while the moon is in the lane its swept strip is marked as a danger and it strikes a ship left on its rail (a fifth of the hull before plating, never the last of it, thrown clear). The gate rows are solid between their rings, so the lane is crossed only through a gate: through a green one (in the moon's lee) nothing minds the party; through an amber one the lead has forced it and that rail's Rail Wardens wake. The moons cross the lane about eleven metres a second apart, inward, so a party that keeps behind the first moon meets each gate as it goes green. Ishen teaches the lights once, with the view drawn back over the first two rails. Two departures from the manifest: the main quest's "cross the Orrery" step is an `Interact` on the far gate's crossing (`FarGate`), because a flag set by a point and not by a script line would only move the step on the next load; and the Names of the Moons are read in Ishen's order (each keystone waits for the one before it), because his three radio lines name them in that order. The chapter's closing card carries the first two sentences of the manifest's body, which in full runs past the card's two lines.
- **D262 - The Glass Choir's rule is the release's first two lines, done with the hands before the engine hall asks for the third (November 12).** The brief gives the dungeon four clamps and the approach to their works; the fight owns the clamps. So the dungeon's rule is the Bridle's **stays**: fibres drawn taut from the clamps' works that pin the dead Choir's ribs across the gallery. A hero takes a stay's weight at its seat; it pulls, then eases, and the rib lifts back into the body (`BridleStay`, guide 15.18). Introduced with nothing awake straight after the release is read at the first clamp, tested where the slack frees two Glass Sentinels held in glass, combined under Fibre Wisps with Clamp Tenders that draw the stay taut again until they are stopped. The fight then adds "let go together". The Choir's first silence needed something the game didn't have: `ZoneDef.quietArrival` (append-only, off everywhere else) stops the arrival bark for a zone, and `BarkService.PlayArrival` honours it. There is no shortcut out of the engine hall: the walk back up the body with every stay slack and the light moving through the glass (`BridleHold`, 15.19) is the chapter's payoff. Map brief: docs/maps/GlassChoir.md.

- **D263 - The first two chapters' towns have people with somewhere to be, and Mira walks on her own stride (November 12).** The Decks, Tallow Drift and Lanternwick stood still beside the towns built since. The Decks and Tallow get theirs from a staging of their own (`TownLife.Apply`, after the chapter 1 stagings, before the ground): one root a rerun replaces, chapter 1 and 2's Vael bodies (never the body of anyone who stands there with a name) in their own dyes, errands on `WalkLoop`, and `Whereabouts` for the story: on the first morning (until the survey key) the galley serves breakfast and two neighbours are home behind the cabins' low walls; after it the queue has become shoppers and a visitor for Orrin, the neighbours are at the market and Hal's bench; from the Cantor's fall cabin 01 stands open while its household packs for the Nacre transfer, and from the homecoming they wait with packs in the market's vestibule. "Resident interiors" became exactly that: the cabins are already seen from above over 1.1 m walls, so people at home in them, and one hatch that opens at a beat with the packer and a crate filling its threshold, so it is never a way in; no new rooms. Lanternwick's are built by its own builder (two of its townsfolk who wore Mira's body now wear Ilo's and Oda's, same spots and dyes; an orchard picker; four errands; the courier square's pair go to watch the archive wall once Hessa is down, the archive's reader goes in among the racks once Ishen has his record). One audit holds all three towns: two metres off every replay route (the chain's and every older one in docs/quality/routes) and story walk, out of the fixed camera's line to the named and to prompts, off floor signs and doorways, not inside a prop, no neighbour in the same body, an errand's line clear of anything solid; the navigation is rebaked with everyone standing and the staging refuses if any route leg's path or any prompt's reach changes. An unprofiled walker's feet hold only at .7 of its body's measured walk (GeneratedAnimator plays the walk at .7 below 1.47 m/s), so every errand walks at that, measured (`CivilianStride`). Mira's walk is read off her own sole in the heroes' convention, played as her own Walk state plays it (without foot IK, where the heroes' states use it; read with it, her walk came out 4% fast): 1.08 m/s, `Resources/Motion/Mira`, where art intake binds it, closing D195's "Not built: Mira's measured walk". Her ScriptedWalk home now plays at .83 of her walk instead of the floor rate .7, and through each stance her planted toe slides .034 m/s instead of .171 (`CivilianStride.Slip`; on the deck `TownLifeTests.MiraPlantsHerFeetOnHerWalkHome` reads .045 against .147, as distance over time summed: a mean of each frame's speed is thrown far out by the test runner's uneven frames).
- **D264 - Chapter 8's ordinary fights are six new modes of old families, and Vessa Korl falls back at half and leaves at nought (November 12).** The brief's eight new kinds and the returning Muster are all existing behaviour families tuned by data (CHAPTER_GUIDE section 5, "Chapter 8 gave six families a mode each"): the Pikeguard is a `Guardian` **pair** (one braces, one covers, and neither strikes across the other, so a hero between them is struck by neither until they step apart round him: Brekk's "get between them" is the answer, made a rule); the Flak Gate an `Anchored` **curtain** of rows on a clock that never follows a ship; the Muster Lancer a `Strafer` that **comes about** slow and open after its lance run and **loses** a ship a rock's `SensorShade` hides for two seconds; the Ash Crawler an `Ambusher` that **lunges**, and every ambusher now reads `GroundHeat` and flees hot ground; the Pour Drone a `Rider` whose pour **stays** burning on its rail; the Forge Hound a **heavy** `Leaper` (knocked down, not shattered, caught in the air) that **scorches** where it lands; the Muster Mortar the guide's own Lobber with `Burn`; the Sensor Buoy an all-round `Anchored` flare inside 8 m, because the picket's engineer draws the sweep and the alarm (docs/maps/PicketOfTeeth.md). Chapter 2's Scavenger, Sergeant and Skiff return as new definitions (`ReachScav`, `ReachSergeant`, `ReachSkiff`) that fight exactly as theirs did at chapter 8's weight: the originals are tuned for Sync 9 and pinned by chapter 2's tests. Vessa Korl is a boss-registered elite (`BossController.VessaKorl.cs`, her sheet's own 50 to 140 s): the writer's barks put her retreat at her shift ("She's pulling back to the heart. Don't follow her blind."), so at half her bar she sets `vask.vessaFallsBack`, says `VessaFallsBack` in front of her squad (an `AfterTalk` that plays mid-fight, `duringFight`), and holds the gantry door to the heart for her second half, pouring the floor across the way to her; at nought she walks out (`Ending.Leaves`) and her pair, the encounter's own, fights on. Her whole bar is fought, so every shared boss test measures the fight a player has. XP is split with the second combat engineer's bosses (4,000 of the 8,625 Sync 22 to 25 costs): the ledger in `HarrowgateBuilder.Enemies.cs` assumes a main quest of 1,000 and side quests of 800 and ends the main path at 24.7, the side quests at 25.0.
- **D265 - The Picket of Teeth shows what it sees, wakes in three stages, and watches only the ship the player flies (November 12).** Chapter 8's space approach (docs/maps/PicketOfTeeth.md). Four sensor buoys each sweep a beam across the lane, eased back and forth over an arc on one shared clock; every rock in the lane is a sensor shadow, a circle at the plane as wide as the rock (`SensorShade`, in Combat so a hunter can ask it too). Both the live beam and, fainter, the whole fan a buoy can sweep are drawn on the plane cut short where a rock stands, so a shadow is a dark wedge in the light (`SensorSweep`). The lead ship in a lit beam fills a meter; full, the picket goes up a stage (`PicketAlarm`): amber (lamps pulse amber, beams sweep a third faster; eight seconds unseen and it is quiet again), a hunter wing off the carrier's deck (broken, or lost for twelve seconds unseen with nothing awake near, and it is amber again), and the carrier (it swings broadside, a second wing, the wall's flak gates wake, marked bursts round the lead while it is in the field, until the party leaves). A buoy shot down gets its last call off (a stage); the route lock's first piece trips the lock's own alarm (at least the hunters), so the quiet way reaches the lock fresh but never takes it apart in silence, as the script's "Irun keeps the hunters off him" asks. The partner's ship and a conversation are never heard (the Spore Veil's lesson), and nothing of the alarm is saved: a load starts quiet. A gauge in the glow gauge's place names the stage and what it brings, and says SEEN or HIDDEN. The quiet route was designed by simulating the sweeps and is held by a test that measures, on the built buoys, a window of at least 1.5 s on every leg and none for a straight run up the middle. The route lock is the generated frame cut in code into the four pieces Idra calls (its posts, its bottom rail, its middle works), each drifting off as its hand is worked; the main quest's "cross the Picket" step is an `Interact` on the line in sight of the lock (`PicketCrossed`), as D261's was, so it moves the moment it is crossed.
- **D266 - The Bridle Foundry's rule is "marked means hot": pours on a clock, a press, and wheels that open one way and arm another (November 12).** Chapter 8's dungeon (docs/maps/BridleFoundry.md; `PourChannel`, `ForgePress`, `CagedChoir`, CHAPTER_GUIDE 15.27). Hot metal is only ever dangerous inside a marking laid before it pours: a tap run (a furnace's run, always pouring, sealed) is a wall until its sluice floods it; a ladle line pours on a fixed timetable a player reads by watching the ladle run out on its rail; a drop press stamps on its clock once armed. The two dungeon wheels each cool one run (the way on) and drain another bed (arming the gangway's ladle, then the press), as Idra says in `IdraFirstSluice`; the heart's three sluices are the boss's (`CoolantSluice`). To keep a marking readable over hot metal from the fixed camera, a pour's strip is wider than its trough so the marking's rim lies on the dark kerb, the metal sits under the marking's lift and is lit only while marked, in pale yellow-white rather than the marking's orange; a test measures the rim from the game's camera and saves the frames. Place damage (a pour, the press) is a share of the bar measured as every hit is, through `Health.Receive` with the chapter's parts, so the hero's plating takes its part: a fifth before plating is about eleven percent at the chapter's gear, an ordinary hit's band. A tap run strikes only while its seal is shut, so a check that opens every seal to walk the floor walks across it as it does deep water. The young road's release plays after `TovraRelease`'s last line rather than under its lines: a world component can't see a conversation's line count. *Look pass, the same day:* the place is hot everywhere a marking isn't (furnaces' mouths and crucibles that breathe, embers, lit smoke, the heart's vents), all of it off the floor; a tap run glows ember, deep red, dimmer and redder than its marking, and only a pouring ladle's metal is the melt's pale yellow-white; where the forge's light reaches the floor it is painted as a soft pale pool. A second camera test lays a marking at every hot place a hero can stand and measures it as the pour's is. The release is walked, not placed: everyone waits out of frame from the fight's start and walks in at the fall.

- **D267 - The Scoria Flows' crust is its own warning, and a marking always draws over it (November 12).** Chapter 8's wild zone (docs/maps/ScoriaFlows.md, guide 15.24, `CrustField`). Each plate of a flow keeps a clock of its own: dark crust (safe), seams lit from cherry red to white heat for two and a half seconds with a tremble and a groan (about to go), then the slabs sink and the melt is open (it burns), then the crust rises dark again. The warning is the crust itself, not a `Telegraph`: a field of plates marked in the house's danger colours would bury every enemy's marking in the same orange, and the zone's rule is that the ground tells you. So the crust is mostly dark (the open melt is rafts of cooling skin with the glow in the cracks between them, held to a low overall light; the seams pass through orange only briefly), is opaque and lies under the height a marking is laid at, and a marking wins by its light; `ScoriaTests.AMarkingOnBrightFlowStillReadsAsAMarking` measures a mortar's circle on open melt against the same circle on the basalt shelf and fails on a plate lit as a plain sheet of a marking's orange. A plate is drawn along its cracks (interlocking with its neighbours) and burns only where its drawn outline and its footprint agree. Open melt is the place's damage (no source): a share of the bar, never the last of it, and the party set down on the last ground the lead stood on that doesn't cycle; a partner caught alone is moved beside the lead and never burnt (it doesn't choose where it stands); an enemy left on open melt burns. Nothing cycles in a conversation or while paused, and nothing is saved. Combat reads it through `GroundHeat` (the Ash Crawler won't hide under hot crust and leaves crust that turns hot).

- **D268 - Every staging step registers itself, and one runner restages a scene (November 12).** Chapters stage into each other's scenes after their builders (a warp on the Tallow approach, chart talks and warps on Many Hands, chapters 1 and 2's whole staging order), and a rebuild of the earlier scene dropped them without a word: chapters 7 and 8 were missing from the built Many Hands city, and the hull carried three copies of chapter 7's warp sign. Each staging entry point now carries `[Restage.Step(id, scenes, After, Leaves, From, ...)]` in its own file, and `Restage.All` (marker `RESTAGE_OK`, `-restage-scenes a,b`) runs them in one order, fingerprints each scene before and after, and refuses on a missing piece; `RestageTests` fails by name on any piece a step declares that a built scene lacks (guide 12.1). The rule is one line: after rebuilding any scene, run `Restage.All` for it. Two steps aren't safe to run twice and run only where what they leave is missing: `RoomDressing.Apply` (its scatter keeps clear of every route file, so a redress after later chapters' routes took about 200 plants off Sorrel and refused Tallow Drift) and `BiomeLandscapeUpgrade.Apply` (its grass avoids whatever stands at the time). `ZoneLook.Apply` stays the zone builder's own step.

- **D269 - The Coronach is a walk of lanterns, heard by stopping, closed by the player, and the market lifts as everything in its footprint (November 12).** Chapter 9's beats 8 and 9 (docs/maps/DecksLeaving.md, "The Coronach" and "The launch"; guide 15.29; `CoronachDoor`, `CoronachWalk`, `CoronachLaunch`). (1) **One visual rule**: a lit hand lantern set down at someone's feet means somebody there still has something to say; heard, they put it out and stay with their door; out of the party's sight they go aboard with it and their door is shut. Guests have no door and no lantern. (2) **No arrow, no counter**: the main quest's step 12 reads "Walk the town. There is no hurry." and targets the zone alone (exact edits in `HomeBuilder.Quests`; the wiring's `CoronachDoor` report is dropped). (3) **Nothing starts by walking past**: every door is a `Stop and listen` prompt (`Say yours` for the three heroes), so a player who stops for nobody hears nothing and loses nothing. (4) **Extras play in the same stop** as their speaker's line, started in the frame the line lets go of the dialogue, so the box never closes between them; someone whose only lines are extras stands there only if one of their stories was finished, where the town is ordinary without them. (5) **All three heroes are in the street**: the two out walk it as the party, the one resting stands at their own place as their own body, which leaves at once if they are brought out. (6) **Orrin waits outside Hal's workshop from the Saint's release** and calls the walk on a crossing across the workshop's open front, so it can't be missed and nobody hunts for him (the Decks' Orrin goes at `home.saintReleased`); **the close is the player's**, `Stand with Orrin` at the walkway joint once the three are heard, never on a timer. (7) **Music by name on the zone**: `ZoneDef.cueMusicIds` (new) stages a moment's own tracks; the walk plays Old Village (measured the sparest of the game's tracks) and the launch Title Theme until the composer's two pieces land, a one-line swap in `HomeBuilder.Coronach`; `BarkService.Hush` (new) keeps every bark quiet from the call to the launch's talk. (8) **The launch** holds the party for its length (about four seconds before Neve's talk, the talk at the player's pace, two and a half after), walks anyone on the market's side back, opens the joints with the game's own hold, tremor and pad, and lifts the market as everything standing between its two collars (chapter 1's hulls are separate pieces; no reparenting of the Decks' scene, batching taken off, still baked): the parting is the market's west collar, a step short of the yellow line, so the line Taren painted goes up with the market. A save between the close and the talk replays the launch; the Ring's arrival saves after it.
- **D270 - A chapter's zones are defined at once, and the ones not yet built stand as placeholders (November 13).** Chapter 9's first engineer defines all six zones on day one so eight engineers can build on one `Define`, and a ZoneDef with no scene made `ContentFrameworkTests` red for every chapter. `HomeBuilder.BuildZones` ends by standing an empty, loadable, lit scene (floor or open plane, `Arrival`, walls) for any chapter 9 zone that has no scene yet, logged `HOME_ZONE_PLACEHOLDER`; a zone's own build replaces it, and a built zone is never touched. Also chapter 9's: Nacre Grave's walls are short overlapping boxes laid along each curve, never `ZoneKit.FlightCorridor`, whose legs run past their ends into the lane at a bend (chapter 6); Oda gets the `CharacterDef` she never had; Abri's turn for Nacre plays at Harrowgate's cells as chapter 8 ends, with the same talk aboard Many Hands for a save that left first.
- **D271 - The game does not carry D3D12's pipeline library between sessions (November 13).** The shutdown crash (QA-031) was the engine serialising that library in the graphics device's destructor after loading it from the last session's file: D3D12Core copied a cached pipeline past the end of mapped memory. One file, grown over twelve replay segments, crashed four exits of the same route in a row and was never rewritten (a session that only reached the title quit cleanly with the same file), so a player could have been left crashing at the end of every real session. `PipelineCacheGuard` (Core, at start, Windows player only) keeps a folder at `<temp cache>/dx12_pso_cache_lib.bin`, so the engine starts a new library each session and cannot write it; the engine logs "Error when writing PSO binary data", which is this and nothing else. What was weighed: the engine's single-file cache avoids the fault too, but its switch (`-force-d3d12-single-pso-disk-cache`) is a command-line flag only (as a `boot.config` line it does nothing), and a store's launch options don't cover a double-clicked exe. Frame times on the Lanternwick route were the same with the library, without it and with the single cache, presumably because the graphics driver keeps its own shader cache (measured on one machine, with that cache warm). `quality-replay.ps1` and `quality-chain.ps1` take `-PlayerArgs` for engine switches; `tools/crash_stack.py` reads a player crash dump's faulting stack with the engine's symbols.
- **D272 - What chapter 3 was like when it was first played, and the rules that came of it (November 13).** Chapter 3 passed its tests and had never been played from a save. The first replay chain through it stopped eight times, and each stop was something a player would have met. The rules, each with the fault that made it:
  - **A prompt is in reach on the floor plan.** Reach was a straight line to the object's middle, so a way out carried on a six-metre arch (Rootmarket's river gate) offered nothing from the place its builder gave to stand at. A prompt is offered inside its range on the plan and no further above or below than that range; the nearest still wins (`PromptService.FindInteraction`).
  - **A cradle always saves.** A hurt party that couldn't pay for the repair was refused outright, with nothing saved. It now saves and says "SAVED, NOT REPAIRED" with the fee.
  - **Only the piloted hull pays for a scrape.** The partner's ship took the 12 a scrape costs and died on barge hulls it was steered into by its own follow.
  - **Cover in flight is the shape it looks.** A barge's box collider stopped shots that passed its pointed bow in clear space. The seed barges carry a convex hull fitted to their outline (`XylosBuilder.FitHull`). Other flight zones' cover has not been audited for the same.
  - **Between chapters the panel, the card and the closing line all point at whoever opens the next one.** After chapter 2 the objective sent the player back to the Reach and the card said the road was "uncharted"; the way on was a second talk with Keeper Abri that nothing mentioned. `SideStories.KeeperOpensAChapter` puts the arrow on her, and chapters 2 and 3's closing words name her.
  - **A hide's ring has an edge, and somebody says what it is.** An ambusher's hide was a pale wash with no line, on a green bank: three occupied hides looked like an empty shore, and nothing told a player that stepping in is how the fight goes on. The ring now ends in a bright faint line (still a boundary by D222, never a danger's outline), and the lead says what it is the first time anything goes under in a save.
  - **A boss's length is sized from its measured share.** The Seedthane fell in 40 to 44 s where its sheet asked 60 to 110, because the replay party got .42 to .46 of flat out against it, not the .3 assumed (it stands planted and its legs take bar with them). Its integrity is 44,000 (27,000 before), which lasted 82 s in the player; its sheet carries the .36 that fight measured, and its ceiling at a quarter of flat out is 130 s. The Floodcrown looked right at 158 and 165 s only while the replay driver stood idle against its flank (it measured its reach to a body's middle, and this body is wider than a blade is long); swinging, it fell in 102 s, a share of .5, and its integrity is 88,000 (62,000 before), which lasted 143 and 158 s. The Rotbloom fell in 91 s (.57) and is 74,000 (54,000 before), not yet fought again. A boss's fight also has to begin: the Rotbloom's waits until the partner is through its gate, and a door walk that ends a stride inside the hall leaves the partner in the doorway (`RouteFightTests` now asks every boss door walk to end three metres in). Every boss after chapter 2 still carries an assumed .3 until the chain has fought it: read its duration, set its share, size its bar (CHAPTER_GUIDE section 10).
  - **What is drawn between the camera and a floor stays under the camera's real line, with room to spare.** The Walking Canopy kept its near walls to a rule (D119) that sat just over the real line of sight, and stood columns on them a metre over it. At the south rim of the Floodcrown's basin that hid the party to the chest and put bark two metres from the lens: the boss fell, and the conversation after it was played, with nobody on screen. The drawn ground there is now its own function, lower than the ground bodies meet (`CanopySeen`), and nothing stands on it. Other zones carved the same way have not been checked.
  - **Somebody behind a shut door is spoken to through it.** In the Drowned Waystation Pim waits behind a grille, and the talk that finds them opens it and lets pump three run. The bars stood 3.9 m in front of Pim and a talk reached 3.2, so nobody could have started it: chapter 4 stopped there for every player. Pim is heard from 6 m. No test had tried it (they set the flag); a check written for the replay routes found it before anyone played it: every press a route makes for a flag or a talk has to have some prompt in reach of where the route stands (`RouteFightTests`).
  - **The clock a hero's moves are timed on never runs backwards.** `GameTime.Now` could step back one float's worth across a hit-stop, which the replay's release trace read as a release before its press.
  What the replay tools learned is in CHAPTER_GUIDE section 10: fights begin inside their boxes (`RouteFacts`, `Route.enter`, `RouteFightTests`), a route leaves a zone from inside its way's reach (`RouteFightTests`, after two routes stood a tenth of a metre outside one), cradles are saved at until the game says so, a walk a `stopWhen` cuts short has arrived, the driver draws ambushers by stepping into their rings, measures its reach to a wide body's side, and rolls out of marked lanes in flight.
- **D273 - A told chapter says where the next one begins (November 14).** The chain's stills showed chapter 4 ending on a bridge in Driftreed with the panel reading "Chapter 4 complete. Many Hands is waiting, and so is a tug with a ledger": no arrow, nothing on the chart, and chapter 5 beginning a chart ride, a lift and a launch away at a dock that had only just appeared. The route knew the way because its engineer did. D272 fixed this for chapters 2 and 3 by name (Keeper Abri); from chapter 4 on the next chapter begins somewhere different each time, so it is data.
  - `QuestDef.next` (a `QuestTarget`, like a step's) is where a main quest's closing line sends the party. While no story quest is open the objective panel aims by it exactly as it aims by a step (`ObjectiveHud.Aim`): the way out toward its scene, and there the person, the report or the point. The chart's diamond stands on its stop, or while that place has never been stood in, on the nearest charted stop along its `via` (`RouteChart.Objective`).
  - Each closing line ends with what to do, in the chapter's own words: chapter 4 "…a dock off Tallow Drift…: launch for it", chapter 5 "read the chart for Hoarfell at the chart table", chapter 6 "…Ishen…: he's on the dock", chapter 7 "Brekk has word from Scoria: he's on Many Hands' dock". Chapter 8 hands on by itself (Abri turns the convoy ten seconds after its card), and chapter 9's is chapter 10's to write.
  - `ContentFrameworkTests.BetweenChaptersThePanelSaysTheToldChaptersLastWordAndItsArrowLeadsToTheNext` stands a party with each chapter told in the zones it could be in and asks for the line, the arrow's target and the diamond. A new chapter adds its hand-off's lines to that list.
- **D274 - A boss's state line that can't fit beside the skill row stands as two (November 14).** The state under a boss bar is the fight's instruction ("A FOOT IS PLANTED  ·  CUT IT"), drawn in one line centred under the bar and kept off the first skill icon by margins, shrinking a little to fit and never under the name's size (D226's follow-up). That was sized on chapter 1 and 2's lines, the longest 35 characters. Nine later lines run 37 to 50, and the first one played, the salvage frame's "CLAMPED TO THE HULL  ·  WAIT FOR A FOOT TO PLANT", ran under the Cleave icon with its last word hidden. Rather than cut nine instructions to a count, a line whose ink is wider than its room at the least size is broken at its dot into two centred lines, what the boss is doing over what to do, hung from where one line stands, and the dark band behind the bar's words comes down 30 units with them (`GameHud.StandState`). `QaNov10HudTests.ABossStateTooLongForItsRoomStandsAsTwoLinesClearOfTheSkillRowAndTheHints` holds every long line clear of the icon, the break meter and the hint bar; a boss with a new long line adds it to that list.
- **D275 - A conversation that holds the controls holds the ships (November 14).** In the Aurora Shear a current carries every hero ship along it, and it went on carrying them through conversations. Idra's call about the tender is five lines: in the first player run the short lane took the party 87 m in the 12.4 s they lasted, through the tender's fight and out the far side, with nobody able to steer, and a player who reads slowly would have gone further. `AuroraCurrent.Carry` now leaves a ship where it is, and its Thrust alone, while input is blocked; the ship still counts as in the current, so the gauge stays lit through the talk (`HoarfellWorldTests.ACurrentHoldsAShipWhereItIsWhileAConversationHoldsTheControls`). The rule for anything else that moves a hero by itself (a tow, a lift, a moving floor, a conveyor): it either stops while a conversation holds the controls, or it is the conversation's own staging and ends where the talk does. D273, D274 and this were each seen wrong in the player; their checks were written against the fixed code and were not run against the old.
- **D276 - The Orrery is crossed on its own time and the Last Warden is fought with the driver's own hands (chapter 7's routes, November 14).** Neither machine can be played honestly by a route alone. The Orrery's gates shut on a clock that starts at the zone's load and pauses with nothing the driver can count, so a route that timed its crossings would force gates as often as not; the driver now waits on the machine itself, `"until": "lee:<n>"`: the gate nearest the lead on rail n is green with its moon gone past and going away, which leaves every rail's gate open eleven seconds or more (green alone does not: a ring goes green a few seconds before its moon comes round). The Warden's bar takes nothing at a held line, so a route that only fought it would fight it for ever; the driver's clamp hand walks to a lit seat, stands beside the clamp's fibre (inside the seat, outside a strike's lane), takes the weight with the interact button and lets the pad go until the clamp opens, and stops a Clamp Tender first, as the Foundry's sluice hand does for its wheel. Both are a person reading the screen and using the pad (`ContinuousReview.Oriel.cs`, CHAPTER_GUIDE section 10; `OrielReplayHandsTests`, which builds each broken state under `ORIEL_HANDS_BROKEN=1`). Neither has been played in a player.
- **D277 - Duskline: tread-hall four comes home on the ride, and the dawn's rules are one component's (November 14).** The Meridian Crawl and the Terminator were two engineers' and met at a contract neither had closed. Settled, one engineer now holding both: the Terminator ends with the ride (the engine car carries the party 110 m out ahead of the light and sets `home.rideDone`), and the hall is home in the Crawl from that flag, not from the third coupling (a party that mated it and left by the chart has brought nothing home). `DorrowTreadHome` plays in the Crawl as an `AfterTalk` on `home.rideDone` while the hall rolls in, and the lip onto its porch opens only once the hall has coupled **and** he has said so (`CouplesIn.shut`): on the flag alone a quick talk opened it onto a porch still rolling in. The run goes south, toward the fixed camera, so the line comes down the screen behind the party; the cars stand west of the way on a flattened drag lane and the way swings seven metres east of each, because a 12 m car beside the way would stand on the camera's line to anyone passing it. The dawn takes enemies through combat's `Dawn`/`DawnTakes` (registered `takes:true`) and burns only heroes; there is no `GroundHeat` or `ShadeCover` for it, and its shade is `DawnLine.Shade`, the casters' shadows at the sun's pitch now. `home.rideDone` joined `HouseLeaves.storyFlags`.
- **D278 - The Works draws its keel under the camera's line, and the Ring's cover is the shape it is drawn (November 14).** D272's two rules, applied to chapter 9's dungeon and its last flight zone. *The Works*: bodies and the bake still meet the keel as `HomeBuilder.Works.Height` has it (the Waystation's rule, six tenths over the line to a hero's feet), but the landform is drawn from `Works.Seen`, the Walking Canopy's rule: a kerb of six tenths on the camera's side of a floor, then a slope four tenths and more under the real line (12.1 m up, 14.97 m back, 20.7 degrees), and nothing within five metres of any floor's lens. The hull plate, pipes and lamps stand on the wall as drawn, and where it is too low for them they aren't hung. The Saint's door trigger covers the floor the fight is fought on, z 174 to 200 (46 x 26 on (0, 187)), not the contract's 46 x 38 on (0, 194), whose middle stood inside the Saint's own capsule. *The Ring*: every district's and wreck's cover is one convex collider fitted to the outline of what is drawn at the plane (`Ring.FitCover`), and a district's spacing footprint is that shape's box (`SectionFlight.footCentre`, `footSize`; `body` is a `Collider` now). Tests: `SeparationWorksTests.WhatIsDrawnOnTheCamerasSideOfAFloorStaysUnderItsRealLineOfSight`, `BrokenRingTests.EveryPieceOfCoverIsTheShapeItIsDrawnAtThePlane`.
- **D279 - Chapter 8's rooms are waited on, never timed, and the Slag Colossus's heart is worked with the driver's own hands (chapter 8's routes, November 14).** The Picket's beams sweep on a clock that starts from the game's, the Flows' crust and the Foundry's ladles and press from the zone's load, and every one stops in conversations, so a route that timed a crossing would cross on luck: the driver now waits on the room itself, `"until": "clear:x,z;x,z"`, true when nothing the room times (a live beam, crust open or rising, a ladle's strip marked or pouring, a press's square marked or coming down) would be on the way from the lead through those points for as long as the lead will be on it, by its own motor's pace with .4 s either side and 15% slack (`ReplayWays`). The Colossus takes nothing while it glows, so a route that only fought it would fight it for ever: with a sluice's seat ring showing the driver walks to the seat, presses interact until its hands are on the wheel and lets the pad be until it opens; a locked target that takes nothing (its plate turns every blow, or its bar is held at a line) with a part of the same boss that can be hurt has the lock flicked across with the d-pad (a quenched plate, a pour-arm), and a bare part is chased with its boss's tells (`ContinuousReview.Rooms.cs`). Each is played through the driver's pad and shown failing first (`HarrowgateReplayTests`): the quiet route flown leg after leg is seen on its first leg, waited on it is never seen; the first tongue walked onto an opening plate threw the lead nineteen times, waited on never; the heart without the hands opened nothing in fourteen seconds, with them a plate was shed. The Picket is crossed the quiet way, B4 (which watches the lock's approach) shot down from its north side first, amber and no more; the lock's own alarm brings one hunter wing, broken south of the flak gates' reach. Nothing of it has been played in a player. *Amended after the first reconnaissance (s44, s45, November 14):* route 44 names the pair chapter 7 leaves out (Taren and Idra) and only puts Taren in the lead; it had brought out Idra, already out, with a step that ends on the frame of the swap, so the analysis read the frame before it. In the Picket each pocket turns the ship's nose down the next leg and gives Idra two seconds to come round behind it: a settle that ran past pocket 9 left the nose south, her place behind it (PartnerBrain: 3 m back, 2 right) was across leg 10, and the lead slid round her hull into Rock 8 and stuck. The rock's sphere is inside what is drawn, and the leg cleared it by the margin it was checked with. The lock is worked from its line, north of the flak curtain's rows and a hull short of the seal (`LOCK_LINE`, z 636.5, read from the gates' line, the marking allowance, Taren's hull and the braked drift): from z 629.4, in the front row, the braked walks to the last three pieces never arrived, every volley rolled the ship back, and the flak brought a defeat (the run's "simulation paused" was that defeat screen). The hunter wing is no longer fought. It launches 120 m off and a Lancer loses a ship it hasn't seen inside 40 m for 2 s, so it loses this one at once, which is the picket's own way back down ("wing lost"). Shown failing first in `HarrowgateReplayTests` with Idra out: leg 10 as flown, and the lock as it was worked. *And after s47 and s48:* the battery's field is crossed down one column of plates each way, with a 75 s wait (its declared way runs down a seam between two columns, two plates underfoot at once, clear 3% of the time southward). The driver's chase on foot walks toward the nearest awake body of a fight when the lock has nothing: the gallery's west pour drone keeps its rail and was behind the lead, out of the lock's reach. It also reads through a conversation that opens in a fight step with a boss's fight underway, as Vessa Korl's fall-back talk does. That talk had stood open 108 s, with her fight's clock stopped under it. *And after s50, s52, s56 (November 14):* both of the driver's own walks are bounded to a short walk (45 m) or the fight the lead stands in, and never taken while another hand has the pad or from a seat a step isn't fighting at. That covers the walk toward a fight's nearest awake body and the older walk to raise a fallen partner. The partner walk is what took route 56's lead off coupling 3's seat, after Idra 160 m back, into the dawn; the new rule never fired there. Route 50's walk to the trophy gallery now stops for Sela's talk, whose line begins at z 99, and the generator fails any walk into a town talk's line that doesn't stop for it. Route 52's landing ends as it arrives, before the town's answer opens.

- **D280 - A conversation can be followed in the same box; a room that stands for one scene isn't baked into the day's paths; a way's goal stands at its end (November 14).** Chapter 9's Coronach and the Decks on the day (docs/maps/DecksLeaving.md). (1) **Follow-on talks.** The Coronach's extras play straight after a door's own line, and the box closed for three frames between them: the second could only be asked once the first had let go. `DialogueSystem.Follow(node,speaker,done)` (asked from World as `Npc.SayNext`, bridged by `WorldUiBridge`) queues a node behind the one running; that one's end leaves the box up and the party held (`LinePresenter` and the completion skip their hide) until the next one's first line takes it over; refused with nothing running; dropped, its `done(false)`, behind a talk cut short. Nothing changes for a talk nobody asks to follow (`CoronachTests.AConversationFollowedOnKeepsTheBoxUpWhereTheOldWayClosedIt` measures the old way's gap first). (2) **The meeting isn't in the day's bake.** DecksLeaving baked its paths with the town meeting standing (forty people, six benches), so the dock office's floor kept those holes all day: Oda's door at the stamping window had no floor in reach on the paths, and the partner would walk round people who had gone aboard. The meeting's room carves its own (a carving `NavMeshObstacle` on each solid) while it stands. So do the walk's people, hidden until the call (baked, the two placed on the walkway bridge left edges a walker could step off): `Decks.Bake` leaves every carving obstacle out. (3) **A way's goal.** Someone a way is walked to (Ilo at the notice board) stands at its end and past it; that way is no fault of theirs, every other way is (the builder's `OffTheWays` and `CoronachTests` read the same rule and the same `Decks.Ways`). (4) **Mira's three talks on the day aren't branched**: each ends in her shop with no choice, so `DialogueInputTests` reads the ending from the lines; manifest 8.2's `book.Branch(node,"shop")` would make it wait for a choice. (5) DecksLeaving's restage step is `From="Hub_Decks"`. (6) **Nothing but the walk is pressable beside it** (afternoon): from the call to the launch the walk switches off the Halo's three docks, Hal's folding bench and console and Tam's still things (`CoronachWalk.shut`; gate six's dock had won the press at Sela's last word and launched the party mid-walk); guests and the people there for their stories are spoken to where they stand, within 2.2 m, and stand where no door, last word or the close has anything else pressable within a metre of it from 1 to 1.75 m off it or beside its person (the build refuses otherwise, `CORONACH_PROMPTS_CROWDED`; `CoronachTests.EachDoorLastWordAndTheCloseIsThePromptOfferedAllRoundIt`).
- **D281 - A boss with parts pays its bar in blows; its parts choose, they never take (November 14, chapter 9's combat).** The Foundry Saint's released arms took a fifth of its bar each and the Debris Cutter's pods a third, from collars and pods carrying 2% and 10% of it: D272's planted-boss fault built in (the Saint's own length model said 71 s). Now a blow on an unlocked collar or a venting pod comes off the bar as a blow on the body does; each line the bar passes takes the part struck most (the player's choice of order stays); a part carries its line's share, so one struck through alone goes at its line. The Saint's press takes nothing while its raised arms bear it, only in its openings (a slam's two collars, the head's lift, a break), and only while it fights: the shared length test measures flat out on a passive body, and a plate there read as a party that can't hurt it. While a pod vents the Cutter keeps it turned on the ship it fired at (its stern mine-layer never came round to the lead). XP settled at the roster's ledger: the Saint 1,600, the Cutter 1,350 (2,000 and 1,800 would have ended the chapter near 27.3).
- **D282 - In the Aurora Shear a current carries everything in it, so a fight there is a running fight down the lane (November 14).** The first player run (nov13b s32-rejected-current-a, -b) moved only the ships: every tender circle and skiff lane landed where a ship had been, neither run took a blow from any fight (the one hit was a tow scrape), and a 30 s chase step was carried 260 m, past `Fast_0` and through `Fast_1` and `Descent_0`. The brief has the skiffs "riding the currents" and the tenders "hauling along the fast lanes", so the currents now carry the Muster too: an enemy within 35 m of the lead (where `EnemyBrain` gives up anyway), every marking, a tender's burning melt and every shot (`AuroraCurrent.Float`). A fight rides as one, everything within 35 m of the lead at the lead's own pace: each body carried by the current where it was, a skiff circling at the lane's eased edge drifted at a third of the ship's pace and out of its own lane (8 blows in 24 s against 15 in still space, lead-own-2); carried as one, 15 against 15, the wing 9 m off at 12 s. Between the bodies in a current a fight plays as in still space, so the roster's tells and answers stand unchanged (a tender's circle out toward it and round its melt; a skiff's lane sideways). The rule stays: riding with it fills the Thrust the rolls need; against it you crawl and the fight goes on without you. Chosen over leading the aim (a skiff's lane can't be predicted for a ship drifting across it) and over still-water fights (rideable past). Not carried: what a hero's skill lays in the world. Combat hooks called only by the currents (`EnemyBrain.Carry`, `EnemyBehaviour.Carried`, `Telegraph.Shift`), so no other zone changes. Routes after a fight in a current never fly back up the lane. Idra's line keeps every shard 8.2 m plus its size off (`ShearZone.TowRoom`; drops none as built). Written and type-checked on the day Unity's licence lapsed: not yet run.
- **D283 - A lane that doesn't exist yet isn't drawn (November 14, chapter 9's way in).** The warps to Nacre Grave at the Picket and off Many Hands' stern stood shut with "No lane to Nacre yet" from the end of chapter 8, and went on saying so once they had opened. They now appear, already open, when Keeper Abri turns the convoy (`home.turnedForNacre`, which her talk sets itself), as chapter 5's dock off Tallow does: a way the story hasn't made is not a locked door. Written and unrun when this was numbered (`HomeWorldTests`).
- **D284 - The Picket's beam throbs at amber (November 14; changes D265).** At amber the beam over the ship pulses on the alarm lamp's own beat, still in its cold colour, because the lamps that say amber stand about 50 m off the lane's middle and are out of frame almost everywhere: the stage has to be read off the thing the player is watching. `HarrowgateWorldTests.EachStageOfTheAlarmIsPhotographedAsTheGameDrawsIt` saves a still of each stage. Built and unseen when this was numbered.
- **D285 - Chapter 10 changes form inside a zone: the berth (November 14).** Mother of Doors and the Tollkeeper are fought partly in flight and partly on foot, with the boss still up. The lead takes the first engineer's design ([ch10-wiring.md](chapters/ch10-wiring.md) section 6.1): a berth is a part of a zone of the other form, entered and left only by a prompt; a save never stands on its ground; a defeat there retries at the fight's door; a conversation holds it still (D275). And its rule 3: a boss whose bar is made of parts keeps the parts it has lost through a defeat and a Continue, which departs from D204 for these two fights only (nobody opens a clamp twice). The alternative, a scene load at every dock, keeps D204 whole and costs four to six scenes, a frozen world while the party is on foot and a boss reset at each dock. Bound: the berth is proved in the practice yards first (`BerthTests`), with every earlier zone's cases unchanged; the fights are built against the contract either way, and if the berth is not proved in its first wave the docks become scene loads that keep rule 3.
- **D286 - In the Returning Passage it is Sela who turns back, and Taren goes with her (November 14).** The party is three with two out, and the story's last act is Sela turning her ship for a tug with Taren beside her. At the distress call the pair out becomes Sela and Taren, said in the scene (Idra takes the convoy's flank and is seen holding it), whoever the player had out; the run before the call is flown by the player's own pair, and the choice returns after the rendezvous. A turn spoken by portrait while two other ships do the flying would give the game's last decision to somebody who isn't there.
- **D287 - The last survey is flown at Sorrel (November 14).** `LastSurvey`'s course opens in SorrelSecondSky on `end.motherFreed`, reachable before the passage and from the neighbourhood's moorings afterwards; its prospect is a chart stop that arrives at the course's start.
- **D288 - Chapter 10 ends on the credits, not a card (November 14).** The last chapter shows no CHAPTER COMPLETE card: the credits roll over the convoy under its new sky and the game comes back to Neighbourhood Afloat, which stays walkable. `ChapterCards` gets a rule for the last chapter, and `TurnBack`'s closing line is what the objective panel says afterwards.
- **D289 - Nothing that turns solid during a fight may wall a hero in (November 14).** The Host Marshal's ranks are put back to sleep where they stand and stay as ice, which is cover from then on (its spear lodges in them). Ranks fight at arm's length, so the three still out when the wall went back to sleep froze round the lead beside a thaw rig, and their boxes and the rig's made a cell: in the first player run he rolled against its walls for the last sixty-five seconds of the fight and stood there through the conversation after it and the walk the route then asked of him. A rank that freezes within a hero's reach (its own half-width, a hero's, and room to walk between two) is now ice to look at and nothing to walk into; one that freezes across the room is cover as before (`BossController.BesideAHero`, `HoarfellCombatTests.RanksThatFreezeRoundAHeroLeaveHimAWayOut`, which first builds the cell the old way and requires it to hold). The rule is for anything a fight makes solid while heroes are in it: a body that becomes scenery, a door that drops, ice, rubble. Either it cannot appear within a hero's reach, or it moves the hero out first. Written on the day Unity's licence lapsed: type-checked, not yet run.
- **D290 - Reconnaissance replays find where a route stops; only a focus-checked replay passes one (November 14).** A replay is a visible player window that is rejected the moment it loses the focus, so no chain could run while an engineer with a shell worked (447 of 1,055 seconds lost that way on November 14), and every hour of play was an hour nobody could build. The cause was narrow: the input system switches the driver's made-up pad off while its window is behind another, so its presses go nowhere. `quality-replay.ps1 -Reconnaissance` and `quality-chain.ps1 -Reconnaissance` start the player with `-quality-reconnaissance` and no window (`-batchmode`; a window was tried first, and one left wholly behind another presents about once a second, a fifteenth of real speed, while the windowless player kept a focus-checked run's pace to the second): the driver's pad is heard whatever has the focus, every other device is switched off for the run (a real pad in use elsewhere would otherwise be heard too), and the clean-timing refusal is waived. What keeps it honest: the run's `run.json` and `analysis.json` always say `"valid": false` with `"reconnaissance": true` and the verdict in `reconnaissancePassed`, so a tool that knows nothing of this refuses it; its log says `QUALITY_RECONNAISSANCE_OK`, never `QUALITY_REPLAY_OK`; the chain writes SCOUTED, never PASS; its folder must have `recon` in its name; `quality_resume.py` lets a reconnaissance run go on from a passed run or from reconnaissance that found no fault, and never lets a run that is to be evidence go on from reconnaissance (`test_quality_resume.py`, four cases); and the analyzer refuses it as timing evidence. A reconnaissance run shares the machine with builders and test mirrors, so before believing a stop read the frame times in its `frames.csv` (a median far over 16.7 ms is the machine, not the game). A reconnaissance chain may also step past a route that found faults, when told to (`-PastFaults`) and when that route ended in the zone its last step expects, so that one run finds every stop in a chapter; the faults stepped over are written into the next segment's `resume.json`. The strict replay is unchanged, and the last whole chain before a commit is still run alone, in focus, with nothing else working.
- **D291 - On a berth a body fights only heroes of its own form (November 14).** With the berth (D285) the zone's form changes under every enemy in it while the party is docked, and a flier read as on foot would sink off the plane and fire at a hero on a shelf he can't reach. Each enemy's form is now read once from where it woke (`EnemyBrain.Flier`: inside a berth's box the berth's, anywhere else the zone's own); a flier never marks or fires at a hero on a shelf and goes home to wait, as for a safe pocket, and a ground body on a shelf leaves the ships alone; a shot or strike already on its way when the party docks or launches passes through a hero of the other form (`Health.Receive`), unless a boss's move is marked to reach the shelf (`Health.AnyForm`); lock-on and the partner pick only bodies of the hero's form. The pad's "enemy awake near" refusal counts only the lead's own form, so a shelf's tenders don't hold the pad: they are why the party docks. Only zones with a berth are touched (`ZoneController.HasBerth`); every other zone reads exactly as before. `BerthTests` builds each case the old way first and requires it to fail.
- **D295 - Warm, the Host Marshal can't be broken (November 14).** Its two first fights for the replay driver had seven breaks each, and a break lets a blow past the warm shell whole and half again, 4.3 times a warm blow: the driver beat it past half with two of three couplings still running and the fight was over in 94.8 s against a two-minute floor (nov14-recon5 r36). While a coupling runs nothing breaks it and its meter stays at nought (`Health.ClearBreak`, called by nothing else); its moves still open it; cold, it breaks as anything does. The warm shell is .3 (.35), a falter 2 s (2.4), a spear caught in ice 2.5 s (3). Laid over that fight's own events, about 125 s for the driver, a share near .5. Its integrity stays at 115,000 and its sheet's share at the measured .6 until a chain measures the new fight: with .6 the shared length test can't be green with the pair ceiling (the same party must be under 1,597 a second flat out for one and over 1,533 for the other, and one measure read it at 1,711 and 1,835 on one build). Also: the steam over each rig was ninety additive puffs that stacked to a white glare hiding whoever stood there (now twenty, faint, and only while the rig runs); the gallery's camera side was checked against D272 (a hero 1.5 m in is seen whole; one at the very edge loses his feet: drawing it as the Canopy's left the dressing floating, so it is left for the look pass, docs/maps/FrozenHost.md "Camera"); put to sleep it sinks 1.1 m and bows 12 degrees from where it stood, its rig held (its animators and the support that stands it on the floor off: that support had taken every frame of the old kneel back), and Taren's line over it is Sad, not Tender.

- **D293 - Chapter 9 is played from its prompts as built, and its waits read the screen (chapter 9's routes, November 14).** `tools/home_routes.py` writes chapter-53 to chapter-61, from Abri's word in Harrowgate to the fleet's chart (`chapter9.complete`). Every prompt a route presses is read out of the built scene (`scene_prompts`: where it is, its reach, the flag it sets, the zone a way goes to), never measured once, and a stand at the Coronach's doors must leave the prompt meant nearer, by twice the walk's tolerance, than every other prompt in reach (the day's docks included). The driver gained four hands, each a person reading the screen (`ContinuousReview.Home.cs`, `HomeReplayHandsTests`, each shown failing first): `"until":"prompt:<words>"` waits for the prompt the game offers (Orrin walks to his board, then to the joint); the pad is let go while the lead's hands are on a tread-hall coupling (a second press was a swing, which let it go); a wreck track is part of a way's `clear:` in flight (Nacre's tow line and berth ring, the Ring's channel); and the Foundry Saint is struck at a planted collar, and climbed with three arms down. `HomeRouteTests` loads each zone in the state the routes walk it (the meeting's room, the Coronach's people, the Works joint by joint) and judges every leg, press and lock. Two gaps closed the same day, found by chapters 6 and 10's route engineers: a press that leaves a zone is judged in the zone it is pressed in (filed under the zone it arrived in, no warp, pad, dock or way of routes 53 to 61 had been judged; `HomeRouteTests.EveryWayOutIsJudgedInTheZoneItIsPressedIn` shows the old filing first), and the generator refuses a lock pressed from wherever the fight before it ended (`Checked.refuse_unknown`), walking or flying into the fight's volume first (`Checked.into`: the Works' control room and gallery, whose second fights share the first's box). None of it has been played in a player.

- **D296 - The last chapter's ordinary enemies are the earlier ones at its own weight, and they work in twos (chapter 10's combat, November 14).** Chapter 9's crews and machines are authored at 126 to 131 and fall under 9% of Idra's bar from Sync 28 in Choirwrought (the Warden's baton is 8.8% at 28, 8.5% at 29), so each comes back under a Stillwater id at 142 to 150, in the same body and with the same name on screen: `StillwaterWarden`, `StillwaterWeldArm`, `StillwaterRig`, `StillwaterInterceptor`, `StillwaterTowDrone`, `StillwaterTender` (chapter 7's Clamp Tender, 96, is 8% at 27), and `ClampGun`, chapter 8's Flak Gate's curtain in its body. Nothing new in code: "working together" is two existing modes and a placing. The Warden fights in chapter 8's pair (one braces, one covers, neither strikes across the other) and still gives ground and stands aside; a clamp's two guns are set crossed, each turned 35 degrees toward the other, so behind one is in front of the other and the only quiet place on the double beat is where their second rows cross. The ledger: Sync 27 to 30 costs 10,500, the main quest pays nothing, the side quests 400, the bosses as assumed 7,000 (Mother of Doors 1,500 as defined; the Tollkeeper 1,600, Meret 1,200, 1,300 and 1,400, theirs to set) and the main path's ordinary fights 3,190; so the party meets the five at Sync 27.1, 28.1, 28.8, 29.1 and 29.5 and ends at 29.9, 30.0 with the side quests. Each boss's sheet should say 27, 28, 29, 29, 29 (`LastMooringCombatTests` holds a sheet to within .6 of the road). Chapter 9's definitions are untouched.
- **D292 - Nobody strikes Mother of Doors: her bar is four clamps opened in order (November 14).** Chapter 10's first boss is a rescue, and Idra's plan says it before anyone asks ("Nobody shoots at her"). In her room (any scene with her `BridleClamp`s, `settledBy` `MotherOfDoors`) she can't be struck or locked onto (`Health.Damageable` false, so she is no target for the partner either); her bar is her four clamps, a quarter each, opened in order 1 to 4 (each clamp's guns wait on the clamp before: one arrow, one next clamp, Iru after the first and the third, the fourth the one that swings on her strain), each the hold from Oriel, eased only in her slack after a move and never with a Clamp Tender at it. Each clamp opened is kept on the disk at once and loads open (D285: `SaveSystem.KeepCheckpoint`, `BridleClamp.keepsOpened`), and she wakes at the line of those still shut, in their phase, with no shift played. Everything her fight uses lies within 34 m of her root at the crater's middle, because the boss bar, the panel's fight line and the ground framing reach no further (`BossController.Focus`) and a boss's specials start within 30 m (hers within 38, `MotherTune.Reach`): Sorrel's seats moved from r 50 to r 23. Each clamp off narrows her sweep's wedges and her wave's bands while she quickens (shorter tells and rests, more bands, the lash from the first, the heave and a back-swing from the third); her blows never grow with her phase. In a yard with no clamps of hers she fights on integrity as any boss (80,000, sized from her measured flat out), so the shared boss tests measure her; her own length is four real docks (`LastMooringBossTests.HerFourCyclesOfFlyDockWorkAndLaunchLastAsHerSheetSays`: 56 s of hers with a near-perfect hand, 152 s with each clamp's guns allowed 24 s; her sheet 120 to 300). Held by `LastMooringBossTests` (her words, her blows, her lanes and how they narrow, cover against her wave, the shelf and its seat, the hold and the Tender, a Continue keeping two clamps open, a conversation holding her and the fourth shelf, her release, her body) and the shared boss tests by her sheet.
- **D294 - A gate that waits for the partner brings her through (November 14).** A fight behind an open gate begins when every hero is through it (`EncounterVolume.TryEnter`), so that the seal never shuts on a companion outside. The partner's place is three metres behind the lead. A lead who stops two steps inside therefore left her short of the line, and the fight never began, with nothing on screen to say why: in the first play of route 41 the Last Warden stood at its engine for seven minutes with both heroes in its hall. The route was mended (its door walk now ends well past the seal), but a player has no route. The gate now records whom it is waiting for once the lead is through, and the partner's brain asks (`PartnerBrain.ComeThrough`, set to `EncounterVolume.Usher`): she walks through abreast of where she stands and stops 2.6 m past the line, ahead of the frame's and the formation's wishes, and the fight begins. Nobody is asked through a gate the lead has not gone through himself. `PartnerComesThroughTests` stands the two in the Warden's hall as that run had them, first with the way through taken away, which must leave the fight unbegun. The rule of D272 for routes (a boss's door walk ends three metres inside its fight's volume) stays, measured from the seal where the seal stands inside the volume.
- **D297 - The last town is hulls moored across open sky, and a counted visit is the person's only prompt while it is wanted (Neighbourhood Afloat, November 14).** Chapter 10's epilogue (`LastMooringBuilder.Neighbourhood`, docs/maps/NeighbourhoodAfloat.md). (1) **Districts, not a landform.** Each district the party knew is a hull's deck at its own make (the Halo's plating and yellow lines, Many Hands' liner tile, Lastlight's flags, Driftreed's reed paths, Lanternwick's cobbles, the Crawl's pavers), and between them is open sky crossed on gangways with a handrail each side. It is one heightfield (30 m down in a gap) whose floor in the gaps and whose walls under 7 m are cut away after the build, so a gap shows the sky and a district's side ends like a hull's; every reachable edge has a rail. What hangs in that sky (ships at their lines, the convoy's hulls, Xylos's islands) hangs under the new sky's giant, as Many Hands' traffic hangs under its star field, so the float check never takes a ship below the quay for a prop on a deck. (2) **A visit and a person are one prompt at a time.** `Homecomings` counts nine points (report `Homecoming`) that stand on nine people who also talk. Two prompts on one body leave A to whichever is nearer, and a visit heard through the Npc is never counted, so while the point is wanted the person's Npc is switched off (`S/World/Homecoming.cs`); heard, the Npc says the visit again, uncounted. The person's finished stories follow whichever opened, in the same box (`Npc.SayNext`), and someone whose only lines are extras stands there only if one was finished. (3) **Every epilogue line is a beat guarded by the zone**, ahead of every older beat (Abri's `AbriManyHands` and others hold anywhere once their chapter is told, and would otherwise play seven weeks on). Ruva's motion nine is her line every time and news only until heard once (`SideStories.HeardAboard` gains `RuvaPassageRules` -> `end.rulesHeard`). Orrin is quiet from `end.handrail` (he is chalking it up).
- **D298 - The replay driver opens a boss the way its bar says, and a lure-stalk costs what it takes (the Rotbloom, November 14).** Chapter-23 stopped at the Rotbloom three times. Its stalks stand on plinths whose only way up is the ramp facing the hall's middle; the chase pushed straight at the nearest from the door, slid round the plinth's steep side into the gap between it and the hall wall and stood there for the round (`ChaseBlocked`'s way began at the navigation nearest the lead, and the nearest to a body in that gap is the plinth's top, 0.6 m over his head, so the "way" was the straight push again); and once a stalk burned, the lock stayed on the shut bloom and ground it at 30% for five minutes without burning a second. Now (`ContinuousReview.Chase.cs`, CHAPTER_GUIDE section 10): the lead's end of a way is taken on his own ground, and so is the far end when it is a body (what is chased, a fallen hero), while a route's point keeps the nearest navigation as before (the 14:26 build took a `findWay` walk's point on its own level too; the mire's third island stands 0.7 m over the route's point, so the way ended 1.5 m short on lower ground and route 23 ran about for 17 s at step 139: `RotbloomDriverTests.AWalkThatFindsItsWayReachesItsPointOnTheThirdIslandsBank`, 84 m and no arrival built that way, 17.3 m and 5.3 s now); a chase on foot follows the floor's way whenever that way bends or climbs, side-steps when even that stops gaining, and gives up for the next reachable thing after 5 s; a shut boss (a blow lands at a share) whose state line or objective names standing things by their card name has those locked with the d-pad, nearest by the floor's way, walked toward when beyond the lock's thirty metres, and struck under the boss's own tells (the driver had never rolled while burning a stalk: it looked for tells on the stalk); an open boss is locked when the lead can reach it before it shuts; and a roll never leaves the ground the lead stands on (off a plinth it goes through the stalk instead). Fought that way, 74,000 with stalks at a tenth fell in 74 s: two stalks were a tenth of its integrity each and took a quarter of its bar each. A stalk is now a quarter of its integrity (`RotbloomTune.StalkIntegrity` .25, as the boss's own header always said: "a quarter of what holds it up"), so the fight is as much work as its bar, which is what the shared ceiling (a quarter of flat out inside five minutes) assumes. That ceiling, at today's flat-out rates (934 to 1,177 a second, QaPlanCombatTests), holds the integrity at 68,000 (74,000 failed it under 987 a second). The door walk ends at x 4, not 2.6: from the landing it passed a metre from the door lamp's post and from the checkpoint 0.4 m (`WendmireRouteTests`). Measured by `RotbloomDriverTests` against flat out in the same run (934 to 986 a second there): 74,000 lasted 161.6 s; 68,000 lasted 100.5, 102.9, 119.0, 119.5 and 150.1 s. The sheet carries .48, the lower where fights differ (CHAPTER_GUIDE section 10), but the median fight is two minutes, a share near .6: at the floor. The two to five minutes and the shared ceiling leave no room for a boss whose replay share runs over .625 (the floor wants integrity of at least 120 x rate x share, the ceiling at most 75 x rate), and the driver here, rolling every tell for a Flash Move and spending two or three EXTRAs, gets about .6 of the flat-out rate that leaves EXTRAs out. Centring it near three minutes would take about 85,000 and a ceiling rate over 1,130 a second. Not settled: the lead's to choose (a ceiling that counts the EXTRA, a floor the sheet lowers for this boss, or a harder hall). The test asserts the fight is no bargain (at least 90 s) and inside the ceiling, and prints the share.
- **D299 - The Tollkeeper's bar is its gate motor, opened a stage a dock; a fight wider than 34 m says how far it reaches (chapter 10's second boss, November 14).** Wiring 6.3 had three braces open the arm once and the motor end the fight; the brief and the lead's plan both ask for a cycle of fly, dock, work and launch that escalates. Each of three cycles is one brace broken in flight (six, the player's choice; a broken brace shortens its outrigger's sweep, the gap the bark names), the arm run out of the gate, a dock, one stage of the motor stopped on foot (a Clamp Tender from the second, the partner's), a launch before the arm's clock (a marked band along the deck) runs out; the bar is the three stages, a third each, and nobody strikes the carrier. From the first stage stopped the cage contracts on a clock its lamps count (a marked fan from each hinge), and the guns' lanes come one, two, three in turn. Every brace and stage is kept the moment it goes (D285): unlike 6.3's "the motor whole", a defeat keeps stopped stages; only a stage the arm's clock ran out on is lost. Its lane is 116 m across and the bar, the panel's line and the ground framing looked within 34 m of a boss's root, so a `Kit` may give its fight a centre and a reach (`BossController.Focus`, `AnyFightUnderway`; unset for every other boss, read exactly as before), and in its room the boss's root stands at the gate under the plane while its body is laid where the carrier lies. Nothing of the cage is solid (D289); a conversation holds its clock, its sweep, the arm and the arm's clock (D275).
- **D300 - The Continuance's flight berth is the space over its own hull's back (November 14, chapter 10's Continuance).** Phase one's door must be within 60 m of its checkpoint, the bay on foot (`EveryBossDoorIsACheckpoint`), and nothing of a deck may show in the flight camera. So the berth (`HullBerth`, plane y 19) lies over the decks themselves, and the hull's back is drawn at y 16 over them: no collider, no shadow, under every ship and over the ground camera, which never rises past 13.5 m over a floor. What stands above the plane (the shelter's wings, the superstructure, the frame gantry) is north of z 246, every lane south of it (`ContinuanceTests.NothingOfTheHullStandsAboveThePlaneOnTheCamerasSideOfTheBerthsLanes`, which first finds a mast stood in the lanes). Cost: the editor's wide stills of the works and the bay look down on the hull's back, so the zone has none. The way to the gantry reads "Dock — The Anchor Chamber's frame gantry", not the wiring's "Dock — The Frame Gantry": a way to a later zone names it.
- **D301 - The credits roll where the game ends, on the talk that ends it (November 14; D288 built).** `S/UI/Credits.cs` rolls only in NeighbourhoodAfloat, and only when a conversation closes having put `game.complete` up, or when the town is entered with it up and `credits.rolled` not (a quit mid-roll): a fixture or a talk that finds the flag already up rolls nothing, so the shared tests that set it (dialogue walks in Hub_Decks, the quest-card walk, the epilogue's own) are untouched. "Then the title" (wiring section 8) is read as the game's wordmark returning over the town as the roll's last image, after which the camera comes home and the party is let go at the stall, the town walkable (D288); the title screen itself knows a finished game by its subtitle, "THE CONVOY IS THROUGH.", once any save holds `game.complete`. Skipping is a machine's mark, not a save's (`Credits.Seen`, kept as the key rebinds are), so a second ending can be skipped; the first never can. The last chapter's card rule is data: a main quest whose `cardFlag` is `game.complete` never closes on a card (`ChapterCards.EndsOnCredits`), and the QUEST COMPLETE card its last talk earns is dropped at the roll's end (`QuestToast.Forget`). The roll credits only what docs/CREDITS.md names, so four of the manifest's headings (writing, the streets, testing, special thanks) are not on it; docs/CREDITS.md gained the fonts, the Sonniss beds and the Staggart shaders, which docs/ASSET_SOURCES.md recorded and it did not.
- **D302 - Meret Venn is three fights that tell one story: the craft that out-flies you, the frame that out-builds you, the woman who will not stop (November 14, chapter 10's last boss).** Three bosses by id, each with its own bar, door and checkpoint (wiring 6.5), every blow authored at 192 to 198 (12 to 16% of any hero at Sync 29 and 30 in Choirwrought) and none growing with her phase: she gets quicker, never angrier. *Ship* (`MeretShip`, flight berth over the Continuance): her shield is up unless she fires through it (her lance) or her tractor pulls; while it is up nothing locks on her and a shot that reaches it comes back down a marked lane at whoever fired it (the first time, the screen says why), so the read and the lunge are the fight; three projectors carry a third of her bar each and the one struck most goes at each third (D281); the Continuance's anchor clamps rise marked through the plating and stand as cover, setting anyone on a seat beside it first (D289); she leaves for the gantry. *Gantry* (`MeretGantry`): the Saint's press, the Works' welding line and the Foundry's sluices, built properly and named as she uses them; her bar is held at each third until an arm coupling opens, and a coupling is opened, never broken, with Oriel's hold while its arm is overreached (a slam on nobody, a weld at full stretch); she takes the lift. *Well* (`MeretWell`): a duel in which every attack is an earlier boss's and is marked; her counter stance turns a blow and answers the first with a marked riposte, then her counter is spent (mashing is punished, a single blow and a roll is rewarded); a clamp closes under anyone who stands still; into her last third she asks her question and listens in a quiet nothing is said over; at nought she yields standing (her rig keeps its stance: not the clip a captain gives up in), `bossdown.MeretWell` is the custody scene's flag, and the person takes over where she stood. Sheets `sync=29`, `driverShare=.3`, bounds 80 to 150 s each. Held by `LastMooringBossTests` (Meret region) and the shared boss tests by her three sheets.
- **D303 - A world behind a flight zone stands inside what the game's camera draws (November 14).** The game's camera draws nothing past 1,000 m; `LookDev` drew to 1,500. Seen from the Orrery, Oriel stood wholly past the far plane: every still of it showed a world no player had ever seen, and in play the approach to a world flew toward empty sky. Scoria behind the Picket had the same fault and was moved inside the same day. Oriel now stands at 165 m radius inside the limit, comes into the frame between rails 4 and 5 and fills half of it at the landing; the gates' rings are drawn slim and open at the top so a ship waiting at one sees the moon go by; a rail's lamps hold green behind each moon for three seconds of travel. Nothing a route flies or waits at moved (the six chapter 7 routes came out byte for byte the same). The rule: a flight zone's backdrop is rendered and judged with `LookDev.Render ... -look-far 1000`, and whatever carries the frame (a world, a moon, a machine) is placed inside it.
- **D307 - Chapter 8's bosses sized on the corrected measure, a duel's yard that faces the duel, and an ambusher that ends its walk on its hide (November 14).** The shared length test's correction (a blade-carrying second hero at the body) read Drask Vaal at 3,655 to 4,348 a second at Sync 24: 86,000 lasted 71 to 77 s against his sheet's 80, and the duel's own test flaked on the same fact (Taren and Idra 78 s in one run). Its pair measure had a fault of its own: every hero was put down facing north, so a lead before his shield faced away from him, and a shooter's aim turns only 120 degrees toward her target; Sela at his shield landed 0 to 6 a second and a pair she led was measured as Idra alone, twice as slow as the rest. Each hero now faces the body and must land a blow a second. With the yard right the pairs are 2,567 to 3,779 a second, so integrity alone sizes him and his shape (shield, openings, shouts) is unchanged: 130,000 (from 86,000) is 115 to 117, 126 to 148 and 138 to 169 s for Taren and Idra, Taren and Sela, Sela and Idra at the placeholder .3, and 100 to 119 s on the shared measure (142 at a quarter, against 200). The Slag Colossus read 2,155 to 2,713 a second there: 82,000 was 121 s against a 120 s floor. It is 100,000, inside a narrow window: 123 s at the highest reading (floor 120), a dark plate broken in 8.7 s at the slowest pair (its dark is 9), the arrival pair's whole fight 186 to 208 s (its own bound 220). Readings at sixty frames still move a fifth from run to run, so it will flake until its shape widens the window (a longer dark, lighter arms) or a chain measures its share (its own model says near .2 of the bare body's rate: a third of its fight is the room). Vessa Korl (60,000: 65 to 74 s against her 50) and the skiff (70,000: 95 to 106 s against 60, 123 s at a quarter against 150) keep their numbers. HarrowgateBossTests' and HarrowgateCombatTests' pair measures count real seconds as the shared one does, so they take its guard: under 50 frames a second a length test is Inconclusive. And an ambusher's walk to its hide no longer steps past it: at a frame of a quarter or a third of a second a 1.5 to 2 m step carried the Ash Crawler from .84 m short to past it and back for ever, and it never went under (harrow-look-10, a starved machine); `EnemyAmbusher` caps the step at what is left, held by `HarrowgateCombatTests.AnAmbusherGoesUnderAtTheHideItWalksToHoweverLongAFrameIs`. No chain has fought any chapter 8 boss yet: each sheet's .3 (the skiff's .29) is a placeholder until one does.
- **D304 - The convoy flows down the Returning Passage and the party flies free of it (November 14, chapter 10).** The passage home is 1,393 m of living bore with 217 groups of the convoy's hulls flowing north round and under the party, every one at least 3 m under the plane, so none can push or hide a hero's ship. No current carries the ships themselves (unlike the Aurora Shear, D282): a current would make the flight back to the tug a crawl against it, and the heroes' own lines would be telling the player to leave the road Orrin has just emptied for them. Everything that moves (the flow, the swimmers alongside, the tow) stops while a conversation holds the controls (D275; `ReturningPassageTests` makes each move with the hold off first). Details and coordinates: `docs/maps/ReturningPassage.md`.
